Arnold Conjecture over Integers Thanks: This material is based upon work supported by the NSF under the Grant No. DMS-1928930, while the first author served as the McDuff postdoctoral fellow at the Simons Laufer Mathematical Sciences Institute (previously known as MSRI) Berkeley, California, during the Fall 2022 semester. Thanks: The second author is supported by NSF DMS-2204321.
Shaoyun Bai, Guangbo Xu

Abstract
. For any closed symplectic manifold, we show that the number of 1 1 -periodic orbits of a nondegenerate Hamiltonian thereon is bounded from below by a version of total Betti number over ℤ {\mathbb{Z}} of the ambient space taking account of the total Betti number over ℚ {\mathbb{Q}} and torsions of all characteristic. The proof is based on constructing a Hamiltonian Floer theory over the Novikov ring with integer coefficients, which generalizes our earlier work for constructing integer-valued Gromov–Witten type invariants. In the course of the construction, we build a Hamiltonian Floer flow category with compatible smooth global Kuranishi charts. This generalizes a recent work of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith, which might be of independent interest.



1.
Introduction

Let
(M,\omega)
be a closed symplectic manifold. Suppose
H:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth function, which is usually referred to as a
1
-periodic Hamiltonian function by identifying
S^{1}={\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
. Denote by
H_{t}
the smooth function obtained by restricting
H
to
\{t\}\times M
. Then the Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H_{t}}
of
H
is a vector field on
M
determined by the formula

\omega(X_{H_{t}},\cdot)=dH_{t}.

A smooth map
x(t):S^{1}\to M
solving the ordinary differential equation

\dot{x}(t)=X_{H_{t}}(x(t))

is called a
1
-periodic orbit of
H
. Write
\phi_{t}:M\to M
the time
t
flow of
X_{H_{t}}
. Then then the set of
1
-periodic orbits of
H
has a one-to-one correspondence with the set of fixed points
\phi_{1}:M\to M
by evaluating
x(t)
at
t=0
. A periodic orbit
x(t)
is called nondegenerate if the linear map

d\phi_{1}:T_{x(0)}M\to T_{x(0)}M

does not have
1
as an eigenvalue. If all
1
-periodic orbits of
H
are nondegenerate, the Hamiltonian
H
is called nondegenerate.

Given the symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
, there is a contractible choice of almost complex structures on
X
which are compatible with
\omega
. The first Chern class of
(M,\omega)
, denoted by
c_{1}(M,\omega)=c_{1}(M)
, is defined to be the first Chern class of
TM
endowed with a choice of, equivalently, any choice of almost complex structure compatible with
\omega
. The minimal Chern number of
(M,\omega)
is defined to be the nonnegative integer
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that the range of the map

\pi_{2}(M)\xrightarrow{\text{Hurewicz}}H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\xrightarrow{c_{1}(M)}\mathbb{Z}

is
N{\mathbb{Z}}\subset{\mathbb{Z}}
. For any
i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N
, introduce the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module

H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}):=\bigoplus_{j\equiv i\text{ mod }2N}H_{j}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}).

Namely, we collapse the natural
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading on
H_{*}(M;\mathbb{Z})
to a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2N
-grading. Because
H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})
is a finitely generated
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module, there exist an integer
b_{i}\geq 0
and a sequence of integers
a_{1}|a_{2}|\cdots|a_{k}
such that

H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{b_{i}}\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/a_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}/a_{k},

where the integers
a_{1},\dots,a_{k}
are the invariant factors of
H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})
as a finitely generated
{\mathbb{Z}}
-module. Using this decomposition, define the quantity

\tau_{i}^{(2N)}(M):=\text{number of invariant factors of }H^{(2N)}_{i}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}).

The main result of this paper is a solution to the homological Arnold conjecture over
{\mathbb{Z}}
for arbitrary closed symplectic manifolds. This bound is similar to the Morse inequality over
{\mathbb{Z}}
which takes into account the torsion part of the homology.

Theorem A
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a closed symplectic manifold with minimal Chern number
N
. Suppose
H:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a nondegenerate
1
-periodic Hamiltonian. Then the number of
1
-periodic orbits of
H
is bounded below by

\text{rank }H_{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}})+2\sum_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2N}\tau_{i}^{(2N)}(M).

(1.1)

We briefly comment on the historical background of the Arnold conjecture. Arnold conjectured (
[
Arn78
, Appendix 9]
,
[
Arn65
]
) that the number of
1
-periodic orbits of any nondegenerate Hamiltonian is at least the minimal number of critical points of a Morse function on
M
(the
strong
Arnold conjecture) and suggested that it is a consequence of a version of Morse inequality. While the strong Arnold conjecture is difficult to prove, there have been significant progresses towards the homological Arnold conjecture, namely, the number of periodic orbits being bounded from below by the total Betti number.
1
1

1

From now on, in this paper, the Arnold conjecture refers to the homological version.
These progresses started with the breakthrough of Conley–Zehnder
[
CZ83
]
, who solved the strong Arnold conjecture for
T^{2n}
using finite dimensional methods. Conley–Zehnder’s method was later extended by Floer to solve the strong Arnold conjecture for surfaces with genus at least
2
and for certain classes of Kähler manifolds
[
Flo86
]
. It was then followed by the revolutionary work of Floer
[
Flo89
]
which invented the Floer homology and established the Arnold conjecture for
(M,\omega)
satisfying
[\omega]=\lambda c_{1}(M)
for some
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
(monotone symplectic manifolds). Floer’s result was extended by Hofer–Salamon
[
HS95
]
and Ono
[
Ono95
]
to cover all semi-positive symplectic manifolds, i.e.,
(M^{2n},\omega)
which does not have
A\in\pi_{2}(M)
such that
\omega(A)>0
and
3-n\leq c_{1}(A)<0
. It is worth noting that these results hold over
\mathbb{Z}
.

For general symplectic manifolds, Fukaya–Ono
[
FO99
]
, Liu–Tian
[
LT98b
]
, and Ruan
[
Rua99
]
proved the homological Arnold conjecture with lower bound coming from the
rational
total Betti number. These papers are based on a kind of abstract machinery, generally called the “virtual technique,” which is designed for generalizing Floer’s construction (along side with the mathematical theory of Gromov–Witten invariants). The most recent advancement towards the homological Arnold conjecture by Abouzaid–Blumberg
[
AB21
]
, which relies more heavily on stable homotopy theory, bounds the number of periodic orbits from below by the sum of Betti numbers in any finite field. In addition to the aforementioned works, using different versions of the virtual technique, the weak Arnold conjecture over rational numbers is reproved in
[
Par16
]
and
[
FW22
]
.

Theorem
A
allows one to obtain a sharper lower bound for the number of
1
-periodic orbits of a given non-degenerate Hamiltonian by treating the torsion components of
H_{*}(M;\mathbb{Z})
uniformly and simultaneously. The lower bound provided by
{\rm rank}H_{*}(M;\mathbb{F}_{p})
as from
[
AB21
]
is also recovered from Theorem
A
by the universal coefficient theorem. Our bound is also strictly stronger than the bound of Abouzaid–Blumberg, for example, when
H_{\rm even}(M)
has only
p
-torsion and
H_{\rm odd}(M)
has only
q
-torsion and
p\neq q
.

1.1.
Proof strategy

The proof of Theorem
A
is based on constructing a version of Hamiltonian Floer homology over the Novikov ring
\Lambda
with integer coefficients and exponents, where

\Lambda:=\Big\{\sum_{i=-m}^{\infty}a_{i}T^{i}\ |\ m\in{\mathbb{Z}},\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}\Big\},

and a comparison with the Morse homology of
M
with
\Lambda
-coefficients using the Piunikhin–Salamon–Schwarz (PSS)
[
PSS96
]
map.

Theorem B
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be a closed symplectic manifold such that
[\omega]
is contained in the image of
H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\to H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
. Assume that the minimal Chern number of
(M,\omega)
is
N
. Suppose
H:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a non-degenerate
1
-periodic Hamiltonian such that the symplectic action of any capped
1
-periodic orbit of
H
takes value in
{\mathbb{Z}}
. After choosing an almost complex structure
J
compatible with
\omega
and some other auxiliary data, there is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2N
-graded chain complex

CF_{*}(H;\Lambda)

freely generated over
\Lambda
by all contractible
1
-periodic orbits of
H
, graded by the Conley–Zehnder index, with differential given by suitably counting stable Floer trajectories
with trivial isotropy group
connecting the
1
-periodic orbits.

Theorem C
.

Let
(M,\omega)
and
H:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}
be the same as in Theorem
B
. Suppose
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth Morse function and let
CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)
be the Morse chain complex associated with
f
equipped with the reduced
{\mathbb{Z}}/2N
-grading. Then there exist a pair of
\Lambda
-linear chain maps

\Psi^{\rm PSS}:CM_{*}(f;\Lambda)\to CF_{*}(H;\Lambda),

\Psi^{\rm SSP}:CF_{*}(H;\Lambda)\to CM_{*}(f;\Lambda),

such that their composition satisfies

\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}=Id+\text{terms with positive $T$-exponent}.

Proof of Theorem
A
.

When
(M,\omega)
and
H
satisfy the assumptions in Theorem
B
, Theorem
C
implies that the induced map on homology

\Psi^{\rm PSS}:H_{*}(M;\Lambda)\to HF_{*}(H;\Lambda)

in an injection. Using the algebraic arguments in Section
4.7
, we see that Theorem
A
holds in this setting.

It is easy to see that if
[\omega]
lies in the image of
H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{Q}})\to H^{2}(M;{\mathbb{R}})
and the symplectic action of
1
-periodic orbits of
H
are all
{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued, Theorem
A
also holds. Indeed, one can suitably rescale the symplectic form
\omega
and the Hamiltonian
H
by a positive integer to reduce to the integral setting, because such a rescaling process does not change the number of
1
-periodic orbits of
H
.

In general, we can choose a sequence of symplectic forms
\{\omega_{k}\}
which represent rational cohomology classes and converge to
\omega
as
k\to\infty
. If
H:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}
is nondegenerate, then for
k
sufficiently large, the
1
-periodic orbits of
H
with respect to
\omega_{k}
has a one-to-one correspondence with the
1
-periodic orbits of
H
with respect to
\omega
. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can assume that
[\omega]
represents a rational cohomology class. Based on the elementary discussion after Hypothesis
4.1
, we can modify
H
to obtain a
1
-periodic Hamiltonian whose
1
-periodic orbits all have
{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued symplectic action without changing the number of
1
-periodic orbits. Thus the theorem follows from the previous discussions.
∎

The proof of Theorem
B
has two major steps, which account for the most important novelties of this paper. First, we show that any moduli space of stable Floer trajectories can be
globally
presented as the zero locus of a continuous section on a
smooth normally complex
orbifold vector bundle over a
smooth normally complex
orbifold, by generalizing a recent result of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith
[
AMS21
]
which works for the moduli space of closed genus
0

J
-holomorphic curves. Moreover, we show that these presentations are coherent, packaged using the language of
flow categories
introduced by Cohen–Jones–Segal
[
CJS95
]
. Second, we apply the
Fukaya–Ono–Parker (FOP) perturbation scheme
introduced in our early work
[
BX22
]
, which was originally proposed by Fukaya–Ono
[
FO97
]
, to obtain
{\mathbb{Z}}
-valued virtual fundamental chains from the aforementioned presentations of the moduli spaces of stable Floer trajectories, and show that the induced algebraic counts from moduli spaces of virtual dimension
0
can be organized to define a chain complex over
\Lambda
freely generated by contractible
1
-periodic orbits of
H
.

The proof of Theorem
C
is quite similar to the proof of Theorem
B
, except that the relevant moduli spaces parametrize solutions of the PSS/SSP equations, which are also known as the “spiked discs.” We package these moduli spaces and their presentations using the language of
flow bimodules
, which are investigated systematically in the symplectic context in the recent work of Abouzaid
[
Abo22
]
.

Next, we give a slightly more detailed description of the technical ingredients.

1.2.
Flow categories and derived orbifold presentation

In this subsection, we discuss about the geometric construction of compatible global charts for the moduli spaces arising from the proof of Theorem
A
.

Given a closed symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
and a nondegenerate
1
-periodic Hamiltonian
H:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}
, following
[
CJS95
]
, one can introduce a topologically enriched category
T^{{\rm Floer}}
such that:

(1)

The objects are capped
1
-periodic orbits of
H
.

(2)

The morphism space from
p
to
q
is the compact Hausdorff topological space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
, the moduli space of stable Floer trajectories connecting
p
and
q
.

(3)

For
p,r,q\in T^{{\rm Floer}}
, the composition map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{rq}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}

is given by the concatenation which forms a broken Floer trajectory breaking at
r
. The composition maps are homeomorphisms onto their images and satisfy the natural associativity relation.

The composition maps naturally equip the space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
with a stratification structure, with strata indexed by words of the form
pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q
. The
pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q
-stratum of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
is homeomorphic to the product

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{r_{l}q}.

The moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
is only an orbispace in general, though the usual Kuranishi reduction process describes it
locally
as the zero locus of a section on an orbifold vector bundle over an orbifold. Moreover, even if every
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
has a global Kuranishi model, its restriction to the boundary stratum
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{r_{l}q}
may not be the product of the global Kuranishi models on each factor. This would obstruct any meaningful inductive construction on
T^{{\rm Floer}}
from these Kuranishi models, leaving alone the issue concerning the
smoothness
of the (thickened) moduli spaces. We solve all of these problems.

Theorem 1.1
.

(See Theorem
4.7
)
Let
(M,\omega)
and
H:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}
be as in Theorem
B
. Then for any capped
1
-periodic orbits
p,q
of
H
such that
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}\neq\emptyset
, there exist a smooth effective normally complex orbifold with corners
{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
with the same stratification structure as
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
, a smooth normally complex orbifold vector bundle
{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
, a continuous section
{\mathcal{S}}_{pq}:{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}
, and a map

\psi_{pq}:{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}

which defines an isomorphism of orbispaces.

We call the quadruple
({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq},\psi_{pq})
a
derived orbifold chart
(D-chart for short) of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
. Given an index
pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q
, one can take the product

({\mathcal{U}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{U}}_{r_{l}q},{\mathcal{E}}_{pr_{1}}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{U}}_{r_{l}q},{\mathcal{S}}_{pr_{1}}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{r_{l}q},\psi_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\psi_{r_{l}q})

(1.2)

which defines a D-chart of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{r_{l}q}
.

Theorem 1.2
.

(See Theorem
4.7
)
The restriction of
({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq},{\mathcal{S}}_{pq},\psi_{pq})
to the stratum of
{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
indexed by
pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q
is isomorphic to the stabilization (Definition
2.3
) of the product chart (
1.2
) by a complex orbifold vector bundle.

If the above data satisfy some further compatibility conditions spelled out in Definition
3.24
, we call this system of derived orbifold charts of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
a (normally complex)
derived orbifold lift
of the flow category
T^{{\rm Floer}}
. The full statement of Theorem
4.7
exactly asserts the existence of an oriented and normally complex derived orbifold lift of
T^{{\rm Floer}}
.

As mentioned above, the construction of derived orbifold chart for a single moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
is based on generalizing a recent result of Abouzaid–McLean–Smith
[
AMS21
, Theorem 1.7]
. We replace their framed
J
-holomorphic spheres by framed
J
-holomorphic cylinders and modify the perturbation method accordingly. The main difficulty is the compatibility mentioned above. To this end, we introduce the
multi-layered thickening
(see Section
5.3.3
) to make sure that the thickened moduli space
{\mathcal{U}}_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{U}}_{r_{l}q}
is truly embedded in
{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
. To endow the space
{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
with a smooth structure, we follow the application of classical smoothing theory from
[
AMS21
]
. However, because
{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
is an orbifold with corners in general, the traditional smoothing theory does not suffice for our purpose. We develop a
relative equivariant smoothing
technique to construct the smoothing in an inductive fashion, which also makes the resulting smooth structures on various moduli spaces compatible.

Remark 1.3
.

We expect that the construction of derived orbifold charts presented in this paper could be useful in the study of symplectic field theory (SFT)
[
EGH00
]
because the methods presented can be used to deal with punctures.

Remark 1.4
.

The derived orbifold chart lift of the flow category
T^{\rm Floer}
should also be applied to construct certain Hamiltonian Floer homotopy theory for a complex-oriented generalized cohomology theory. As a first application, these geometric constructions should simplify the definition of Floer Morava
K
-theory
[
AB21
]
because the algebraic gluing of local virtual fundamental chains should be largely simplified given these global charts.

Remark 1.5
.

We would like to remark that there are some other technical issues one needs to resolve in order to carry out our perturbation scheme on the derived orbifold charts. For instance, to make sure an inductive scheme could work, one needs to guarantee that any prescribed construction on the “boundary statum” (
1.2
) can be extended to a neighborhood of it in
({\mathcal{U}}_{pq},{\mathcal{E}}_{pq})
. To this end, a suitable collar structure is necessary and one has to keep track of the compatibility of such collar structures with the “difference bundle” appearing in Theorem
1.2
. Considerations of this form partially account for the length of the later sections, because we will need a more refined geometric construction compared to the one in
[
AMS21
]
.

Given a Morse function
f:M\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a Riemannian metric
g
on
M
, if the pair
(f,g)
satisfies certain properties stronger than the usual Morse–Smale condition, the work of Wehrheim
[
Weh12
]
shows that the moduli spaces of unpamatrized gradient flow lines of
f
with respect to
g
are compact smooth manifolds with corners. Therefore, one can associate
(f,g)
with a flow category
T^{\rm Morse}
such that:

(1)

The objects are “capped” critical points of
f
, see Section
4.2
.

(2)

The morphism space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{{\rm Morse}}
is given by the moduli space of unparametrized gradient flow lines connecting the underlying critical points of
x
and
y
if the cappings are the same, otherwise it is the empty set.

(3)

The composition

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xz}^{{\rm Morse}}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{zy}^{{\rm Morse}}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{xy}^{{\rm Morse}}

is defined to be the concatenation of gradient flow lines, which is a diffeomorphism onto its image.

To relate the two flow categories
T^{\rm Floer}
and
T^{\rm Morse}
, we introduce the PSS and SSP
flow bimodules

M^{\rm PSS}
and
M^{\rm SSP}
. For instance,
M^{\rm PSS}
is described by the following data:

(1)

For
x\in T^{\rm Morse}
and
p\in T^{\rm Floer}
, we associate the pair
(x,p)
with the moduli space of solutions to PSS equations
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp}
connecting
x
and
p
.

(2)

The flow category
T^{\rm Morse}
acts on
M^{\rm PSS}
on the left, i.e., there is a map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm Morse}_{xy}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{yp}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp}

satisfying an associativity condition. Geometrically, this map is again given by concatenating solutions to gradient flow line equations and Floer-type equations, and it is a homeomorphism onto its image.

(3)

The flow category
T^{\rm Floer}
acts on
M^{\rm PSS}
on the right in a similar fashion, i.e., there is a map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{\rm PSS}_{xq}\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm Floer}}_{qp}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}^{{\rm PSS}}_{xp}

satisfying an associativity condition.

(4)

The right and left actions should satisfy certain associativity relation.

The flow bimodule
M^{\rm SSP}
is characterized similarly. Just like flow categories, one can define the notion of derived orbifold lift for flow bimodules (Definition
3.25
). It turns out that we can indeed construct a derived orbifold lift for both
M^{\rm PSS}
and
M^{\rm SSP}
via geometric arguments.

Theorem 1.6
.

(See Theorem
4.8
)
Each of the flow bimodules
M^{\rm PSS}
and
M^{\rm SSP}
admits a derived orbifold lift (which extends the D-chart lift of
T^{\rm Floer}
and the trivial lift of
T^{\rm Morse}
).

In addition to these statements, the geometric underpinning of Theorem
C
also needs certain moduli spaces designed for interpolating between
\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}
and the identity map on
H_{*}(M;\Lambda)
. Such a structure should be better thought of as a morphism between two flow bimodules “parametrized” by the interval
[-1,1]
, as exploited in detail in
[
Abo22
]
. We do not follow this viewpoint in this paper. Instead, we simply list out the necessary geometric input in Section
4
for the proof of Theorem
C
.

1.3.
Fukaya–Ono–Parker perturbations

Now we discuss about the perturbation methods adapted in the course of the proof of Theorem
A
.

The perturbation scheme to be used in our construction was proposed by Fukaya–Ono
[
FO97
]
and further developed by B. Parker
[
Par13
]
. So this scheme will be referred to as the FOP perturbation scheme. We give a detailed introduction to its idea. For complete details, the reader could consult
[
BX22
]
. In a moduli problem related to pseudo-holomorphic curves, objects (such as stable maps) may have nontrivial automorphism groups; in the case of Hamiltonian Floer theory, the sphere bubbles in a stable Floer trajectory may be multiple covers carrying nontrivial automorphisms. This means that the moduli spaces have certain orbifold-type behavior. It is well-known that transversality could not always be achieved in the orbifold setting. To define invariants using abstract perturbations (see
[
FO99
]

[
LT98a
]
), one needs to use “multi-valued perturbations” and count of transverse zeroes with
{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued weights. Such constructions result in
{\mathbb{Q}}
-valued invariants or chain complexes with
{\mathbb{Q}}
-coefficients. Another more algebraic reasoning to explain the appearance of
{\mathbb{Q}}
is that Poincaré duality for oriented orbifolds only holds over
{\mathbb{Q}}
(see the discussions of Pardon
[
Par16
]
). These two perspectives could account for the reasons why Gromov–Witten invariants and Hamiltonian Floer homology of a general symplectic manifold are only defined over the field of rational numbers.

Let us set up a finite-dimensional model to illustrate the failure of equivariant transversality and the proposal of Fukaya–Ono. Consider a compact smooth manifold
U
acted on by a finite group
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
and
{\bm{W}}
is a representation of
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
. We may regard the orbifold
U/{\mathit{\Gamma}}
as the moduli space and the orbifold vector bundle
(U\times{\bm{W}})/{\mathit{\Gamma}}
as the obstruction bundle. To simplify the exposition even further, let us assume
{\rm dim}U={\rm dim}{\bm{W}}
. In this case, a weighted count of the zeroes of an equivariant transverse maps
S:U\to{\bm{W}}
will give an Euler number, which is an invariant of the obstruction bundle over the orbifold
U/{\mathit{\Gamma}}
. However, the equivariance of
S
implies that
S^{-1}(0)
may contain higher dimensional pieces; for example, when
{\bm{W}}
contains no trivial subreprsentations, the fixed point locus
U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
must be contained in
S^{-1}(0)
. Hence ordinary transversality cannot be achieved equivariantly in general.

The existence of a
normal complex structure
allows one to consider a more delicate kind of section so that one form of equivariant transversality
can
hold. In the above finite-dimensional model, consider the normal bundle
NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
over the
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
-fixed point set
U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
. Meanwhile, consider the decomposition

{\bm{W}}=\mathring{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\oplus\check{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}

where
\mathring{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
is the direct sum of trivial subrepresentations and
\check{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
is the direct sum of nontrivial irreducible subrepresentations. A normal complex structure in this case is a
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
-equivariant complex structure on both
NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
and
\check{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
. To illustrate the FOP perturbation scheme, assume for simplicity that
NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
is trivial with fiber a complex
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
-representation
{\bm{V}}
and
\mathring{\bm{W}}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}=\{0\}
. A section
S
defined near
U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
can then be regarded as a map

f:U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to C^{\infty}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})^{\mathit{\Gamma}},

where
C^{\infty}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
denotes the space of smooth
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
-equivariant maps from
{\bm{V}}
to
{\bm{W}}
. In
[
FO97
]
Fukaya–Ono proposed to consider sections corresponding to fiberwise polynomial maps

f:U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}),

i.e., sections of
E
whose restriction to each normal fiber is an equivariant complex polynomial map of degree at most
d
. Assume
U=U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\times{\bm{V}}
and denote the corresponding section by
S_{f}:U\to{\bm{W}}
. Then one has

S_{f}^{-1}(0)=U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\cup\big(S_{f}^{-1}(0)\cap(U\setminus U^{\mathit{\Gamma}})\big).

Although the zero locus of
S_{f}
still contains
U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
, Fukaya–Ono asserted that the count of the second component (modulo
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
), is an invariant, if
d
is sufficiently large and
f
is generic. If this is true, one could indeed define an integer-valued “Euler number” by counting the zeroes of
S_{f}
with trivial stabilizer.

A difficulty to implement Fukaya–Ono’s idea is that we need to introduce a new notion of transversality for those fiberwise polynomial maps. Certain delicate properties are needed for this transversality notion, especially when we change the cut-off degree
d
for polynomial maps and when we change the isotropy groups. The crucial idea in the preprint
[
Par13
]
of Brett Parker which addresses these difficulties played an important role in the recent construction of the authors
[
BX22
]
. In short, one can define a canonical notion of transversality for those normally polynomial perturbations which behaves well when we change the degree
d
and the isotropy group and which is satisfifed by generic such perturbations such that the integral counting is well-defined as proposed in
[
FO97
]
.

Going back to the Floer-theoretic construction, given a derived orbifold lift of the flow category
T^{\rm Floer}
, we need to construct the FOP perturbation on all the thickened moduli spaces inductively. Although such a construction is cumbersome and somewhat routine, one novelty showing up is the
multiplicativity
of the FOP perturbation.

Theorem 1.7
.

(See Corollary 2.8)
Let
({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i})
,
i=1,2
be normally complex derived orbifold charts (Definition
2.1
) such that
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
is a strongly transverse FOP section (Definition
2.7
). Then the product section

{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}:{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2}

is also a strongly transverse FOP section.

The upshot of the inductive construction of FOP perturbations can be summarized schematically as follows, after defining the relevant counts using
0
-dimensional moduli spaces.

Theorem 1.8
.

(See Theorem
3.49
)
Using the derived orbifold lift of
T^{\rm Floer}
, one can define the chain complex
CF_{*}(H;\Lambda)
from Theorem
B
after choosing a compatible family of strongly transverse FOP perturbations. Similarly, the chain maps
\Psi^{\rm PSS}
,
\Psi^{\rm SSP}
, and the homotopy

\Psi^{\rm SSP}\circ\Psi^{\rm PSS}=Id+O(T)

can be constructed by choosing a compatible family of strongly transverse FOP perturbations on the derived orbifold lifts of the relevant moduli spaces.

As a consequence, Theorem
B
and Theorem
C
hold.

Remark 1.9
.

We would like to point out that the definition of FOP sections depend on an extra structure which is called a
straightening
(see Definition
3.31
). Heuristically, it consists of a suitable Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{U}}_{pq}
and a special form of connection on
{\mathcal{E}}_{pq}
for each of the derived orbifold charts appearing in the derived orbifold lift. Again, there is an issue of compatibility with various structures on our derived orbifold lift. Although the relevant compatibility conditions will be shown to hold after a somewhat routine and cumbersome induction construction, pinning down the correct formulations seems to be a nontrivial task.

1.4.
Discussions

We would like to comment on the implications of our result and some notable features of the techniques.

1.4.1.
Towards stronger forms of the Arnold conjecture

One important quantity in the statement of Theorem
A
is the minimal Chern number. In the extremal case when
N=1
, if the only torsion components of
H_{*}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})
are
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
and
{\mathbb{Z}}/3
, and both of them have odd degree, then they together contribute
2
to the quantity (
1.1
). In other words, our lower bound is only as strong as the lower bound from
[
AB21
]
in this special case. This might suggest that the sharp lower bound in the Arnold conjecture might need to incorporate certain symplectic information from the ambient symplectic manifold, as witnessed by the minimal Chern number in our statement.

On the other hand, it is possible that the methods developed in this paper might eventually resolve the
strong
Arnold conjecture for simply connected closed symplectic manifolds of dimension
\geq 6
. Indeed, following the bifurcation methods initiated in
[
Flo88
]
, it should be possible to study the simple homotopy type of our integral Floer chain complex, which in turn is closely related to the
stable
Morse number of the ambient manifold. When the ambient manifold satisfies the condition at the beginning of this paragraph, the stable Morse number actually coincides with the Morse number.

1.4.2.
Stable/normal complex structure

As emphasized in the exposition of FOP perturbations, the normal complex structure of derived orbifold charts plays a crucial role even in the definition of these polynomial-like perturbations. The closely related notion of stable complex structures also plays an important role in the construction of Floer homotopy type in
[
AB21
]
, in which the stable complex structures are necessary for applying Poibcaré duality to the Morava K-theory of the classifying space of orbifolds. These structures are not necessary for the proof of Arnold conjecture over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. It is natural to expect more applications which make essential use of the normal/stable complex structures on the moduli spaces.

1.4.3.
Algebraic structures and operations

Following the concept of “flow multimodules” as developed in
[
Abo22
]
, one should be able to define multiplicative structure on the integral Floer homology. For instance, it should be possible to generalize the definition of quantum Steenrod operations on Hamiltonian Floer theory of semi-positive symplectic manifolds
[
Wil20
]
to all symplectic manifolds using the
{\mathbb{F}}_{p}
-reduction of our Hamiltonian Floer homology. Our construction of derived orbifold lifts should also be useful for regularizing moduli spaces of
J
-holomorphic curves originated from the algebraic structures.

1.5.
Outline

The following describes the content of every section.

•

In Section
2
, we review the differential topology related to the perturbation scheme used in this paper. Most notably, the multiplicativity of the FOP strongly transverse condition is derived in Corollary
2.14
.

•

Section
3
is devoted to the discussions of flow categories, flow bimodules and their derived orbifold lifts. We show that for a flow category whose derived orbifold lift has a normal complex structure, it is possible to construct a family of compatible strongly transverse FOP perturbations so that one can define a chain complex over
\Lambda
from these data if there is a further compatible orientation structure. The discussions culminate at Theorem
3.49
. A similar result for flow bimodules is presented as well.

•

We bring back concrete symplectic geometry in Section
4
. In particular, we describe the flow categories
T^{\rm Floer}
,
T^{\rm Morse}
, and flow bimodules
M^{\rm PSS}
,
M^{\rm SSP}
, and the structures of the interpolating moduli spaces in detail. After presenting the main geometric statements in Section
4.5
and the necessary input from a
1
-parameter family of PSS/SSP moduli spaces (“the chain homotopy moduli spaces”) in Section
4.6
, we prove our main theorem.

•

In Section
5
, we show how to construct a compatible family of
topological
global Kuranishi charts for the moduli spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
.

•

In Section
6
, We discuss how to use (relative) equivariant smoothing theory to endow the global Kuranishi charts constructed in Section
5
with smooth structures. Moreover, we describe how to construct a normally complex lift of all derived orbifold lifts.

•

The parallel constructions for PSS, SSP type moduli spaces as in Section
5
and Section
6
are presented in Section
7
. Because most of the arguments are only a matter of cosmetic modification, most of the proofs are only sketched.

Remark 1.10
.

We were informed by Semon Rezchikov on September 14, 2022 that he had an independent approach towards similar results following our early work
[
BX22
]
.

2.
Recap of the FOP natural transformation and multiplicativity

In this section we briefly review the construction of
[
BX22
]
concerning the Fukaya–Ono–Parker perturbations and prove an additional property of FOP perturbations regarding the multiplicativity. Some necessary ingredients from the theory of Whitney stratifications are provided in Appendix
A
.

2.1.
Orbifolds and the canonical Whitney stratification on
\mathcal{Z}_{d}

Now we describe the setup for FOP perturbations. In this section we consider effective
2
2

2

A stabilization can make a non-effective orbifold effective.
orbifolds only. An
n
-dimensional
orbifold
is a locally compact, Hausdorff, and second countable topological space
{\mathcal{U}}
equipped with an atlas of orbifold charts: each chart is of the form
C=(U,{\mathit{\Gamma}},\psi)
where
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
is a finite group with an effective linear action on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is an invariant open subset, and
\psi:U\to{\mathcal{U}}
is a
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
-invariant continuous map such that the induced map
U/{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is a homeomorphism onto an open subset of
{\mathcal{U}}
. There is a compatibility requirement for overlapping charts which we will not recall here. In notation the map
\psi
is often suppressed and we identify its image with
U/{\mathit{\Gamma}}
. Similarly, over an orbifold
{\mathcal{U}}
, an orbifold vector bundle consists of an orbifold
{\mathcal{E}}
, a continuous map
\pi_{\mathcal{E}}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
, and an atlas of bundle charts of
{\mathcal{E}}
. A bundle chart consists of a chart
C=(U,{\mathit{\Gamma}})
of the base
{\mathcal{U}}
, a
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
-equivariant vector bundle
\pi:E\to U
, and a homeomorphism
E/{\mathit{\Gamma}}\cong\pi_{\mathcal{E}}^{-1}(U/{\mathit{\Gamma}})
which is compatible with the projections. We denote by the triple
({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U)
a bundle chart. We ofter use charts which are centered at certain points of the orbifold. For each point
x\in{\mathcal{U}}
, an orbifold chart
centered at

x
is a chart
C_{x}=(U_{x},{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},\psi_{x})
where
U_{x}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is an invariant open neighborhood of the origin, such that
\psi_{x}(0)=x
. A bundle chart for
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
centered at
x
is then denoted by
({\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},E_{x},U_{x})
where
({\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},U_{x})
is an orbifold chart centered at
x
and
E_{x}\to U_{x}
is a
{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}
-equivariant vector bundle.

We need a few other frequently used notations. Let
({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U)
be a bundle chart. For each subgroup
G\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}}
, denote by
U^{G}\subset U
the fixed point locus of the induced
G
-action and denote by
NU^{G}
the normal bundle of
U^{G}\hookrightarrow U
. Then the fibers of
NU^{G}
are representations of
G
whose decompositions into the direct sum of irreducible representations contain no trivial summand. We can also decompose the restriction

E|_{U^{G}}=\mathring{E}^{G}\oplus\check{E}^{G}

where
\mathring{E}^{G}\subset E
is the subbundle whose fibers are the maximal trivial subrepresentations and
\check{E}^{G}
is the complement, whose fibers are direct sums of nontrivial irreducible representations.

2.1.1.
Derived orbifold charts

We recall the notion of derived orbifold charts introduced in
[
Par21
, Section 5]
and used in
[
BX22
]
, as well as a few related concepts. We will soon generalize this notion to the case of orbifolds with faces but this generalization is straightforward.

Definition 2.1
.

(1)

An (effective)
derived orbifold chart
(
D-chart
for short) is a triple
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
where
{\mathcal{U}}
is an effective orbifold,
{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
is an orbifold vector bundle, and
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is a continuous section. We say the triple
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is compact if
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
is compact.

(2)

A (smooth)
perturbation
of a compact derived orbifold chart
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is a smooth section
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
such that there exists a precompact open neighborhood
{\mathcal{D}}
of
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
and a continuous norm on
{\mathcal{E}}
such that

\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}({\mathcal{U}}\setminus{\mathcal{D}})}<\inf_{x\in{\mathcal{U}}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}}|{\mathcal{S}}(x)|.

In particular,
({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)
is still contained in
{\mathcal{D}}
and hence
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
is also a compact derived orbifold chart.

(3)

A
normal complex structure
on a derived orbifold chart
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
consists of, for each bundle chart
({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U)
and for each subgroup
G\subseteq{\mathit{\Gamma}}
, a
G
-invariant complex structure
I_{G}
on
NU^{G}
and a
G
-invariant complex structure
J_{G}
on
\check{E}^{G}
. Moreover, these complex structures are compatible in the following sense.

•

Within the same chart, for each pair of subgroups
H\subset G\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}}
for which we have
H
-equivariant inclusions

\displaystyle\ NU^{H}|_{U^{G}}\subset NU^{G},\

\displaystyle\ \check{E}^{H}|_{U^{G}}\subset\check{E}^{G}

we require that they are complex linear with respect to the complex structures
I_{G}
,
J_{G}
and
I_{H},J_{H}
.

•

The system of invariant complex structures are compatible with chart embeddings.

(4)

A
straightening
of a derived orbifold chart
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
consists of a Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{U}}
and a connection on
{\mathcal{E}}
satisfying the following conditions.

•

For each chart
(U,E,{\mathit{\Gamma}})
, the pullback Riemannian metric
g_{U}
is “straightened.” Namely, for each subgroup
G\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}}
, near
U^{G}
, the ambient Riemannian metric
g_{U}
agrees with the bundle metric on
NU^{G}
induced by
g_{U}
via the exponential map along the normal directions.

•

For each chart
(U,E,{\mathit{\Gamma}})
, the pullback connection
\nabla^{E}
on
E
is “straightened.” Namely, for each subgroup
G\subset{\mathit{\Gamma}}
, we can identify a neighborhood of
U^{G}
with a neighborhood of the zero section of
NU^{G}
using the exponential map associated with
g_{U}
. After identifying
E|_{NU^{G}}
with the pullback of
E|_{U^{G}}
using the projection
NU^{G}\to U^{G}
and the parallel transport along normal geodesics using
\nabla^{E}
, the connection
\nabla^{E}
agrees with the pullback connection of the restriction of
\nabla^{E}
to
U^{G}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

[
BX22
, Lemma 3.15, Lemma 3.20]
For a compact derived orbifold chart
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
there exists a straightening in a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
.

There are several natural relations between derived orbifold charts.

Definition 2.3
.

(1)

An
open embedding
from a derived orbifold chart
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
to
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
consists of an open embedding
\phi:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
of orbifolds and a bundle isomorphism
\widehat{\phi}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}|_{\phi({\mathcal{U}})}
covering
\phi
such that
\widehat{\phi}\circ{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\circ\phi
and
({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\subset\phi({\mathcal{U}})
.

(2)

A
germ
of open embeddings from a derived orbifold chart
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
to
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
is an equivalence class of open embeddings from an open neighborhood of
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
with the restrictions of
{\mathcal{E}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
over it, to
({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})
, where the equivalence relation is induced by shrinking the neighborhood. Such a germ is called to induce a
germ equivalence
.

(3)

The
product
of derived orbifold charts
({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i})
,
i=1,\ldots,k
, is the derived orbifold chart

({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times\cdots\times{\mathcal{U}}_{k},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{k},{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{k}).

(4)

Let
{\mathcal{C}}=({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
be a derived orbifold chart and
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
be another orbifold vector bundle. The
stabilization
of
{\mathcal{C}}
by
{\mathcal{F}}
is the chart

{\rm Stab}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{C}})=({\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}},\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}\oplus\tau_{\mathcal{F}})

where
\tau_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
is the tautological section.

Remark 2.4
.

The stabilization operation intertwines with many other constructions. For example, suppose
\phi:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
is an open embedding and
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
is a orbifold vector bundle. Denote by
{\mathcal{F}}
the pullback bundle
\phi^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
. Then there is an obvious extension of
\phi
which defines an open embedding from
{\rm Stab}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{C}})
into
{\rm Stab}_{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\prime})
. On the other hand, if
{\mathcal{C}}
is equipped with a straightening, one can endow the stabilization
{\rm Stab}_{\mathcal{F}}({\mathcal{C}})
with a straightening naturally once
{\mathcal{F}}
is equipped with a bundle metric and a compatible connection.

2.1.2.
Equivariant polynomial maps

To describe the use of FOP sections we need to recall some basic properties of equivariant polynomial maps. Let
G
be a finite group and
{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}
be finite dimensional complex
G
-representations. Let
{\rm Poly}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
be the space of equivariant complex polynomial maps from
V
to
W
, and for each nonnegative integer
d
, let
{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\subset{\rm Poly}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
be the subspace of maps with degree at most
d
. There is a natural
G
-equivariant evaluation map

{\rm ev}:{\bm{V}}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\to{\bm{W}}

whose zero locus is denoted by

Z:=Z_{d}^{G}:=Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}).

(2.1)

This is a complex algebraic variety.

The application of FOP sections crucially relies on the existence of certain Whitney stratifications on the above variety. By a classical theorem of Whitney
[
Whi65
]
, any complex algebraic subvariety inside a smooth variety admits a canonical Whitney stratification (see Theorem
A.4
) whose strata are smooth algebraic submanifolds. However, for the variety
Z
(
2.1
), this canonical Whitney stratification may not respect the group action. One needs to use a “more symmetric” Whitney stratification with a few nice properties—this was the observation of B. Parker
[
Par13
]
. The necessity of having such nice properties comes from the consideration that we need to consider the Whitney stratifications on the variety
Z
for different cut-off degrees
d
and different groups
G
. In our previous work
[
BX22
, Theorem 4.3]
we proved the following result showing the existence of certain canonical “symmetric” Whitney stratifications which have the desired nice properties.

Theorem 2.5
.

There exists a unique Whitney stratification on
Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
subject to the following conditions.

(1)

For each subgroup
H\subset G
, let
{\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\subset{\bm{V}}
be the subset of points whose stabilizer is exactly
H
. Then for each
x\in Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\cap({\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}))
, the germ through
x
is contained in
{\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
.

(2)

The Whitney stratification is the minimal one among all which satisfy the above condition.

Moreover, this Whitney stratification enjoys the following additional properties.

(1)

It is
G
-invariant.

(2)

It is induced from a Whitney prestratification (see Definition
A.1
and
A.2
) on
Z
whose strata are all algebraic submanifolds.

(3)

It is invariant under all
G
-equivariant diffeomorphisms of
{\bm{V}}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
which preserve
Z
.

The nice behaviors of the canonical Whitney stratification allow us to extend our consideration to bundles. Let
B
be a smooth manifold acted on trivially by a finite group
G
. Let
V,W\to B
be smooth
G
-equivariant complex vector bundles with fibers isomorphic to representations
{\bm{V}}
and
{\bm{W}}
respectively. Such datum defines a locally trivial bundle

{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)\to B

whose fiber at
x\in B
is the space
{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x})
. Then there is a subbundle

{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W)\subset V\oplus{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)

whose fiber at
x
is the zero locus associated with
{\rm ev}:V_{x}\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x})\to W_{x}
. Using the invariance property of the canonical Whitney stratification of
Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
under
G
-equivariant diffeomorphisms, the canonical Whitney stratification on the fibers can be “patched together” to define a canonical Whitney stratification on the fiber bundle
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
which is “locally trivial.”

Another statement relevant to us, which was originally proved by Fukaya–Ono, says that when
d
is sufficiently large, the variety
Z
is a union of smooth pieces. For a proof, the readers could refer to
[
BX22
, Proposition 4.9]
.

Theorem 2.6
.

For
d
sufficiently large, for each subgroup
H\subset G
, the locus

Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})_{H}:=Z_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\cap({\bm{V}}_{H}^{*}\cap{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}))

is a smooth algebraic submanifold of complex dimension

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}({\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})+\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathring{\bm{V}}^{H}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathring{\bm{W}}^{H}.

2.1.3.
FOP sections and strong transversality

Now we recall the notion of normally complex sections (which we call by FOP sections, owing credit to Fukaya–Ono and B. Parker), which is Parker’s generalization of the notion of normally polynomial sections.

We first consider the case for a single chart. Let
B
be a smooth manifold,
G
be a finite group trivially acting on
B
, and
\pi_{V}:V\to B
,
\pi_{W}:W\to B
be
G
-equivariant complex vector bundles. Choose a positive integer
d
. Then there is a vector bundle

{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)\to B

whose fiber over each
b\in B
is the vector space
{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x})
of
G
-equivariant polynomial maps with degree at most
d
. Let
V_{\epsilon}\subset V
be an open
G
-invariant disk subbundle with respect to an auxiliary bundle metric. Consider smooth sections of the pullback bundle
\pi_{V}^{*}W\to V_{\epsilon}
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
s:V_{\epsilon}\to\pi_{V}^{*}W
be a smooth
G
-equivariant section.

(1)

s
is called a
normally polynomial section
of degree at most
d
if its restriction to each fiber
V_{x}\cap V_{\epsilon}
coincides with the restriction of an element of
{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V_{x},W_{x})
.

(2)

s
is called a
normally complex section
of degree at most
d
if for each
(x,v)\in V_{\epsilon}
, there exists a smooth
G
-equivariant bundle map
f:V_{\epsilon}\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
such that for points
(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})
near
(x,v)
, one has

s(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})=f(x^{\prime},v^{\prime})(v^{\prime}).

The map
f
is called a
local lift
of
s
near
(x,v)
. We also call a normally complex section an
FOP section
.

(3)

An FOP section
s
is called
strongly transverse
at
(x,v)\in V_{\epsilon}
if for any local lift
f:V\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
near
(x,v)
, the graph of
f
, as a submanifold of the total space of
V_{\epsilon}\oplus{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
, is transverse to the canonical Whitney stratification of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
near
(x,v)
, i.e., the graph of
f
is transverse to all the strata of the canonical Whitney (pre)stratification of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
.

With the above preparations, now we consider the global situation for a normally complex derived orbifold chart.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
be a compact normally complex derived orbifold chart equipped with a straightening.

(1)

A section
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is called an
FOP section
if for each bundle chart
({\mathit{\Gamma}},E,U)
for which
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
lifts to a
{\mathit{\Gamma}}
-equivariant section
S:U\to E
, the following condition is true. Over
U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
we can decompose
E|_{U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}=\mathring{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\oplus\check{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
. The straightening induces an identification of a tubular neighborhood of
U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
with a disk bundle
N_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
inside the normal bundle
NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
as well as an equivariant bundle isomorphism

E|_{N_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}\cong\pi_{NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}^{*}\mathring{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\oplus\pi_{NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}^{*}\check{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}.

Then with respect to this splitting we can decompose

S|_{N_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}=(\mathring{S},\check{S}).

We require that
\check{S}:N_{\epsilon}U^{\mathit{\Gamma}}\to\pi_{NU^{\mathit{\Gamma}}}^{*}\check{E}^{\mathit{\Gamma}}
is an FOP section.

(2)

An
FOP perturbation
of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is a smooth perturbation
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
(see Definition
2.1
) such that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
is an FOP section near its zero locus.

(3)

An FOP section
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
is called
strongly transverse
at
x\in{\mathcal{U}}
if the following conditions are satisfied. Let
({\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x},U_{x},E_{x})
be a bundle chart centered at
x
. By shrinking the chart we assume that
U_{x}
is identified with a disk bundle
N_{\epsilon}U_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}
of the normal bundle
NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}
of
U_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
lifts to an equivariant section
S_{x}:U_{x}\to E_{x}
. With respect to the splitting (induced from the straightening)

E_{x}\cong\pi_{NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}}^{*}\mathring{E}_{x}\oplus\pi_{NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}}^{*}\check{E}_{x}

we write
S_{x}=(\mathring{S}_{x},\check{S}_{x})
. Then we require that there exists an local lift
f:N_{\epsilon}U_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}})
of
\check{S}_{x}
such that the induced bundle map

(\mathring{S}_{x},{\rm graph}f):N_{\epsilon}U_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\to\mathring{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\oplus(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}\oplus{\rm Poly}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}))

is transverse to the subbundle
\{0\}\oplus{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}})
with respect to the canonical Whitney stratification at the point
0\in U_{x}
.

Remark 2.9
.

The strong transversality condition of FOP sections presented as above
a priori
depends on the choices of bundle charts and the cut-off degree
d
appearing in
{\rm Poly}_{d}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}}(NU_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}},\check{E}_{x}^{{\mathit{\Gamma}}_{x}})
. The most important output from
[
BX22
, Section 4]
is showing that the canonical Whitney stratification on
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G}(V,W)
is compatible with the change of the cut-off degree
d
and the group
G
, when interpreted suitably. The upshot is, once fixing a straightening datum of
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
, the strong transversality condition is in fact intrinsic. The readers could refer to
[
BX22
]
for details, and we continue our discussions with such background in mind.

It is a general fact that generic smooth maps are transverse to a given Whitney stratified object. As a consequence, a generic FOP section is strongly transverse. We formulate this fact as the following Proposition.

Proposition 2.10
.

[
BX22
, Proposition 6.4]

Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
be a compact normally complex derived orbifold chart equipped with a straightening. Fix a continuous norm on
{\mathcal{E}}
. Fix a precompact open neighborhood
{\mathcal{D}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
of
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
.

(1)

(Absolute version)
Given
\epsilon>0
, there exists a smooth section
{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
satisfying the following conditions.

(a)

{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}
is an FOP section in a neighborhood of
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
and it is strongly transverse near
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
.

(b)

\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}_{\epsilon}\|_{C^{0}({\mathcal{D}})}<\epsilon
.

(2)

(Relative version)
More generally, let
K\subset{\mathcal{U}}
be a compact subset,
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
be an open neighborhood of
K
. Suppose we are given a section
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{E}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}
which is an FOP section and strongly transverse near
K\cap\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. Then there exists a smooth perturbation
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
which is an FOP section and strongly transverse near
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, such that
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
coincides with
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}
near
K
. In addition, if
\epsilon>0
is sufficiently small and
\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}}<\epsilon
, then we can choose
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
such that
\|{\mathcal{S}}-{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}}<2\epsilon
.

The relative version of the above proposition is often referred to as a “CUDV” type statement. This means that a good perturbation has been constructed on an open neighborhood
U
of a closed subset
C
and we would like to find a good perturbation on a neighborhood of
C\cup D
where
D
is another closed subset, while we want to maintain the original perturbation near
C
and do not change anything outside an open neighborhood
V
of
D\setminus C
.

Moreover, as proposed by Fukaya–Ono, the isotropy free part of the zero locus of a strongly transverse FOP section should induce a homology class.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
be an oriented (i.e., both
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{E}}
are oriented) and compact normally complex derived orbifold chart equipped with a straightening. Let
{\mathcal{U}}^{*}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
be the manifold part, i.e., the open and dense subset of points whose isotropy groups are trivial. Then the following is true.

(1)

Let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
be a strongly transverse FOP perturbation. Then the set
({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}^{*}
is an oriented smooth submanifold of
{\mathcal{U}}^{*}
of real dimension being
{\rm dim}{\mathcal{U}}-{\rm rank}{\mathcal{E}}
and the inclusion map
({\mathcal{S}}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}^{*}\hookrightarrow{\mathcal{U}}
is an oriented pseudocycle, hence represents an integral homology class.
3
3

3

In
[
BX22
]
we extend the notion of pseudocycles in manifolds to general Thom–Mather stratified spaces (including orbifolds) and proved that they represent integral homology classes.

(2)

The resulting homology class, called the
FOP Euler class
, denoted by

\chi^{\rm FOP}({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})\in H_{*}({\mathcal{U}};{\mathbb{Z}}),

is independent of the choice of strongly transverse FOP perturbations and is independent of the choice of straightening, and hence is an invariant of the normally complex derived orbifold chart.

Remark 2.12
.

In fact there exist a collection of homology classes associated to a derived orbifold chart indexed by a finite group and a pair of complex representations. The FOP Euler class in Proposition
2.11
is the leading one in this collection.

2.2.
FOP sections and products

In this subsection we prove that the product of strongly transverse FOP sections is still strongly transverse. This is a necessary ingredient for the inductive construction of perturbations in Floer theory. Moreover, we show that the natural transformation
{\mathcal{F}OP}_{\gamma}
from the stably complex derived orbifold bordism
\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},{\rm der}}
to the integral homology constructed by
[
BX22
, Theorem 1.4]
is multiplicative.

2.2.1.
Products of transverse FOP sections

We fix our notations. Let
G_{i}
,
i=1,2
be finite groups. Let
{\bm{V}}_{i},{\bm{W}}_{i}
be complex
G_{i}
-representations. Then
{\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2}
and
{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}
are
G_{1}\times G_{2}
representations under the product action. Choose nonnegative integers
d,d_{1},d_{2}
such that
d\geq d_{1},d_{2}
. Consider the space

{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}).

It has a subspace

{\rm Poly}_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1})\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2}).

One also has the inclusion of
Z
-varieties

Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1})\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2})\subset Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}).

Abbreviate the three
Z
-varieties as
Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}
,
Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}
, and
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
respectively.

Proposition 2.13
.

When
d_{1},d_{2}
are sufficiently large, the inclusion

\phi:\big({\bm{V}}_{1}\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1})\big)\times\big({\bm{V}}_{2}\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2})\big)\\
\hookrightarrow({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})

is transverse to all the strata of the canonical Whitney stratification on
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
. Moreover, the inclusion pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification on the target to the canonical (product) Whitney stratification on the domain.

The proof is given in Subsection
2.3
.

Corollary 2.14
.

Let
({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i})
,
i=1,2
be normally complex derived orbifold charts such that
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
is a strongly transverse FOP section. Then the product section

{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}:{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2}

is also a strongly transverse FOP section.

Proof.

As transversality is defined locally, it suffices to restrict our consideration to local charts. Let
(U_{i},E_{i},G_{i})
be a bundle chart of
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
centered at
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
such that
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
is pulled back to a
G_{i}
-equivariant section

S_{i}:U_{i}\to E_{i}.

Suppose
NU_{i}^{G_{i}}
is trivial with fiber
{\bm{V}}_{i}
and
E_{i}
is trivial with fiber
{\bm{W}}_{i}
. Decompose
{\bm{W}}_{i}=\mathring{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}}\oplus\check{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}}
. Then near
NU_{i}^{G_{i}}
we can write

S_{i}=(\mathring{S}_{i},\check{S}_{i}).

By the definition of FOP sections, there exists a local lift of
\check{S}_{i}
near the origin

f_{i}:U_{i}^{G_{i}}\times{\bm{V}}_{i}\to{\rm Poly}_{d_{i}}^{G_{i}}({\bm{V}}_{i},\check{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}}).

By assumption,
\mathring{S}_{i}
is transverse to
0\in\mathring{\bm{W}}_{i}
(in the usual sense). Denote
U=U_{1}\times U_{2}
,
{\bm{W}}={\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}
. Then

\mathring{\bm{W}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}=\mathring{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{1}}\oplus\mathring{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}}.

Hence
\mathring{S}=(\mathring{S}_{1},\mathring{S}_{2})
is transverse to
0\in\mathring{\bm{W}}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
and

\mathring{S}^{-1}(0)=\mathring{S}_{1}^{-1}(0)\times\mathring{S}_{2}^{-1}(0).

Hence we may assume that
\mathring{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{1}}=0
,
\mathring{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}}=0
. Next, the graph of
f_{i}
is transverse to
U_{i}^{G_{i}}\times Z_{d_{i}}^{G_{i}}({\bm{V}}_{i},\check{\bm{W}}_{i}^{G_{i}})
. Notice that

{\rm graph}(f)={\rm graph}(f_{1}\times f_{2})={\rm graph}(f_{1})\times{\rm graph}(f_{2}).

It follows that the graph of
f
is transverse to the product Whitney stratification on
U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},\check{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{2}})\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},\check{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}})
. If we view
f
as a map

f:U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times{\bm{V}}_{1}\times{\bm{V}}_{2}\to{\rm Poly}_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},\check{\bm{W}}_{1}^{G_{1}}\oplus\check{\bm{W}}_{2}^{G_{2}}),

it is easy to see that the intersection between
{\rm graph}(f)
and
U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
is contained in
U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}
. By Proposition
2.13
, as the inclusion
Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}\hookrightarrow Z_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
respects the canonical Whitney stratifications, we see that
{\rm graph}(f)
is transverse to all the strata of
U^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}\times Z_{{\rm max}(d_{1},d_{2})}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
. By definition, this means that
S=S_{1}\times S_{2}
is strongly transverse at
x=(x_{1},x_{2})
.
∎

2.2.2.
Multiplicativity of the FOP natural transformation

A consequence of previous discussions is that the FOP natural transformation defined in
[
BX22
]
is natural with respect to products. Although this result will not be used in the setting of Floer theory, we include it here.

Let us recall the relevant notations. An
isotropy type
is a triple
(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
where
G
is a finite group,
{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}
are finite-dimensional complex
G
-representations which do not contain trivial
G
-summands. A
stable isotropy type
is an equivalence classes of isotropy types with respect to the equivalence relation generated by

(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})\sim(G,{\bm{V}}\oplus{\bm{R}},{\bm{W}}\oplus{\bm{R}})

where
{\bm{R}}
is a nontrivial irreducible complex representation of
G
. A stable isotropy type is denoted by
\gamma
or
[G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}}]
if
(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
represents it. To discuss products, we define the multiplication of (stable) isotropy types in the obvious way:

[G_{1},{\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1}]\times[G_{2},{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2}]:=[G_{1}\times G_{2},{\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2}].

The pseudocycles defined by strongly transverse FOP sections induce natural transformations of generalized homology theories. First, for a topological space
Y
one can define the stably complex derived orbifold bordism group

\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y)

generated by isomorphism classed of quadruples
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f)
where
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is a compact stably complex derived orbifold chart and
f:{\mathcal{U}}\to Y
is a continuous map, modulo the equivalence relations generated by

(1)

(
Restriction
)
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f)\sim({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
if
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathcal{U}}
is an open neighborhood of
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(0)
and
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{E}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}={\mathcal{S}}|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}
, and
f^{\prime}=f|_{{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}}
.

(2)

(
Stabilization
)
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f)\sim({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
if
{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime}
is equal to the total space of a vector bundle
\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{U}}
,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
,
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}=\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}\oplus\tau_{\mathcal{F}}
where
\tau_{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{F}}\to\pi_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}{\mathcal{F}}
is the tautological section, and
f^{\prime}=f\circ\pi_{\mathcal{F}}
.

(3)

(
Cobordism
)
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}},f)\sim({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime},{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
if there is a bordism between them extending the stable complex structures.

A stable complex structure on
({\mathcal{U}},{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{S}})
is roughly a lift of the virtual bundle
T{\mathcal{U}}-{\mathcal{E}}
to a complex virtual bundle. Disjoint union of derived orbifold charts induces the group structure on
\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y)
. For details of the relevant terminologies, the readers could refer to
[
BX22
, Section 7]
. For each stable isotropy type
\gamma
represented by
(G,{\bm{V}},{\bm{W}})
, denote
n_{\gamma}={\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}{\bm{V}}-{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}{\bm{W}}
. Then in
[
BX22
]
we constructed a natural transformation of generalized homology theories made of linear maps (see
[
BX22
, Theorem 1.4]
)

\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma}^{Y}:\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y)\to H_{*-n_{\gamma}}(Y;{\mathbb{Z}}).

The main consequence of the fact that strong transversality of FOP perturbations is preserved after taking products regarding the stably complex derived bordism theory is the following theorem.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
be topological spaces and
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
be stable isotropy types. Then the follow diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y_{1})\times\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{1}}^{Y_{1}}\times\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{2}}^{Y_{2}}}
\textstyle{H_{*-n_{\gamma_{1}}}(Y_{1};{\mathbb{Z}})\times H_{*-n_{\gamma_{2}}}(Y_{2};{\mathbb{Z}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{\mathbb{C},{\rm der}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{FOP}^{Y_{1}\times Y_{2}}_{\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}}}
\textstyle{H_{*-n_{\gamma_{1}}-n_{\gamma_{2}}}(Y_{1}\times Y_{2};{\mathbb{Z}}).}

(2.2)

Here the left vertical arrow is the map induced by product of derived orbifold charts:

({\mathcal{U}}_{1},{\mathcal{E}}_{1},{\mathcal{S}}_{1},f_{1})\times({\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{2},f_{2})\mapsto({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2},f_{1}\times f_{2})

and the right vertical arrow is the Eilenberg–Zilber map.

Proof.

Let
({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i},f_{i})
,
i=1,2
be a representative of an element of
\overline{\Omega}{}_{*}^{{\mathbb{C}},\rm der}(Y_{i})
. By the definition of
\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{i}}^{Y_{i}}
, we choose straightenings on
({\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i})
and choose strongly transverse FOP sections
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}^{\prime}:{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
which is
C^{0}
-close to
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}
. Then
{\mathcal{S}}_{i}^{\prime}
defines a pseudocycle in
{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
which is contained in the closure of the stratum
{\mathcal{U}}_{i,\gamma_{i}}\subset{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
, i.e., the set of points
x_{i}\in{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
whose stabilizers are isomorphic to
G_{i}
and for a bundle chart
(G_{i},E_{i},U_{i})
centered at
x_{i}
, the stable isotropy type defined by
(G_{i},(NU^{G_{i}})_{x_{i}},(\check{E}_{i})_{x_{i}})
lies in the class
\gamma
. Then

\mathcal{FOP}_{\gamma_{i}}^{Y_{i}}([{\mathcal{U}}_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},{\mathcal{S}}_{i},f_{i}])=(f_{i})_{*}[\overline{({\mathcal{S}}_{i}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap\mathcal{U}_{i,\gamma_{i}}}].

Now consider the product chart
({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{E}}_{2},{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2},f_{1}\times f_{2})
. The chosen straightenings produce a straightening on the product, with respect to which the product
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime}
is an FOP section. Corollary
2.14
implies that
{\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime}
is also a strongly transverse FOP section and
({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)=({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\times({\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)
. Restricting the product
{\mathcal{U}}_{1,\gamma_{1}}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2,\gamma_{2}}
, one has, as sets

({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime}\boxplus{\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap({\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2})_{\gamma_{1}\times\gamma_{2}}=\big(({\mathcal{S}}_{1}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}_{1,\gamma_{1}}\big)\times\big(({\mathcal{S}}_{2}^{\prime})^{-1}(0)\cap{\mathcal{U}}_{2,\gamma_{2}}\big).

As the product of pseudocycles is still a pseudocycle and the homology classes represented by pseudocycles respect such product structures, therefore the commutativity of the diagram (
2.2
) follows. ∎

2.3.
Proof of Proposition
2.13

First we consider the canonical Whitney stratification on
Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}
. Abbreviate

{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}={\bm{V}}_{i}\times{\rm Poly}_{d_{i}}^{G_{i}}({\bm{V}}_{i},{\bm{W}}_{i}).

{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}
is endowed with the action prestratification, i.e.,

{\mathcal{Y}}_{i}=\bigsqcup_{H_{i}\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}}{\bm{V}}_{i,H_{i}}^{*}

We explain the notations. For each subgroup
H_{i}\subset G_{i}
,
{\bm{V}}_{i,H_{i}}^{*}\subset{\bm{V}}_{i}
is the set of points whose stabilizers are exactly
H_{i}
. The symbol
{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}
denotes the set of all subgroups of
G_{i}
for which
{\bm{V}}_{i,H_{i}}^{*}\neq\emptyset
. Then on the product
{\mathcal{Y}}:={\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
which has the
G_{1}\times G_{2}
-action, the strata of the action prestratification is indexed exactly by
{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\times{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}
and

{\mathcal{Y}}=\bigsqcup_{(H_{1},H_{2})\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{1}\times{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}}{\bm{V}}_{1,H_{1}}^{*}\times{\bm{V}}_{2,H_{2}}^{*}.

The strata of this prestratification are all algebraic submanifolds. Hence by
[
BX22
, Theorem A.21]
, the variety
Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}
has a canonical Whitney stratification, which is the minimal Whitney stratification respecting the action prestratification; by Proposition
A.6
of Appendix
A
, this Whitney stratification is the product of the canonical Whitney stratifications on
Z_{d_{1}}^{G_{1}}
and
Z_{d_{2}}^{G_{2}}
.

Now we prove Proposition
2.13
. As the canonical Whitney stratification on
Z_{d}^{G}
respects the inclusion
Z_{d}^{G}\hookrightarrow Z_{d^{\prime}}^{G}
for
d\leq d^{\prime}
(see
[
BX22
, Theorem 4.12]
), one may assume that
d_{1}=d_{2}=d
. The inclusion map sends
Z_{d}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d}^{G_{2}}
into
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
.

The proof of the following lemma is analogous to that of
[
BX22
, Lemma 4.14]
.

Lemma 2.16
.

There exists a map

\psi:({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})\to{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}

satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

\psi\circ\phi
is the identity map on
{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}
.

(2)

For each
(v,P)\in({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})
,
{\rm ev}(\psi(v,P))={\rm ev}(v,P)
.

(3)

\phi\circ\psi
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
and pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification to itself.

Proof.

For each
P\in{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})
, denote its
{\bm{W}}_{1}
-component by
P_{1}
and its
{\bm{W}}_{2}
-component by
P_{2}
. Then we can regard
P_{1}
as a
G_{2}
-invariant polynomial map

P_{1}\in{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\bm{W}}_{1}))

and regard
P_{2}
as a
G_{1}
-invariant polynomial map

P_{2}\in{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}}({\bm{V}}_{1},{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{2})).

Then for
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2}
, define

\psi(v,P)=\psi(v_{1},v_{2},P_{1},P_{2})=\Big((v_{1},P_{1}(\cdot,v_{2})),(v_{2},P_{2}(v_{1},\cdot))\Big)\in{\mathcal{Y}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{Y}}_{2}.

Then it is easy to verify that
\psi\circ\phi={\rm Id}
and that

{\rm ev}(\psi(v,P))={\rm ev}(v,P).

(2.3)

Now we prove the last property. Consider the manifold
B={\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})
over which there are the trivial bundles
V=B\times({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})
and
W=B\times({\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})
. Then there are two bundle maps

\underline{\smash{f}}_{1},\underline{\smash{f}}_{2}:V\to{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V,W)\cong B\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})

where (here the first variable is the fiber coordinate and the second variable is the base coordinate)

\displaystyle\ \underline{\smash{f}}_{1}(v,P)=(P,P),\

\displaystyle\ \underline{\smash{f}}_{2}(v,P)=(\underline{\smash{\phi}}(\psi(v,P)),P)

where
\underline{\smash{\phi}}(v,P)=P
. Then (
2.3
) implies that

{\rm graph}(\underline{\smash{f}}_{1}-\underline{\smash{f}}_{2})\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}(V,W).

As the identity map of
({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2})\times{\rm Poly}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}({\bm{V}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{V}}_{2},{\bm{W}}_{1}\oplus{\bm{W}}_{2})
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
, which means that the graph of
\underline{\smash{f}}_{1}
is transverse to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
, by a lemma of B. Parker (see
[
Par13
, Lemma 4.10]
, also
[
BX22
, Lemma 4.7]
), the graph of
\underline{\smash{f}}_{2}
is also transverse to
{\mathcal{Z}}_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
, implying that
\phi\circ\psi
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
. Moreover, as the identity map pulls back any Whitney stratification to itself, by
[
BX22
, Lemma 4.8]
, the map
\phi\circ\psi
also pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification on
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
to itself.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.13
.

As
\phi\circ\psi
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
, it follows that
\phi
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
along the image of
\psi
. As
\psi
is surjective, it follows that
\phi
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}\times G_{2}}
everywhere. Moreover, as
\psi\circ\phi
is the identity, it follows that
\psi
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d}^{G_{2}}
along the image of
\phi
. Moreover, as
\psi=\psi\circ\phi\circ\psi
, which implies that the image of
d\psi
at any point is equal to the image of
d\psi
at some point in
{\rm Im}(\phi)
. Hence
\psi
is transverse to
Z_{d}^{G_{1}}\times Z_{d}^{G_{2}}
everywhere.

The proof of the claim that
\phi
resp.
\psi
pulls back the canonical Whitney stratification to the canonical one is similar to the proof of
[
BX22
, Theorem 4.12]
, which relies crucially on a property of minimal Whitney stratifications (see
[
BX22
, Lemma A.11]
).
∎

3.
Abstract constructions of chain complexes and maps over the integers

In this section we provide an abstract recipe of constructing chain complexes associated to flow categories and chain maps associated to flow bimodules. We explain the list of necessary structures on flow categories and flow bimodules which allow one to use FOP perturbations to define the algebraic counts over the integers. This section also serves as a source of notations. In Subsection
3.1
we set up the notations for partially ordered sets and abstract stratified spaces. In Subsection
3.2
we introduce the abstract notion of topological flow categories and flow bimodules. In Subsection
3.4
we define the notion of derived orbifold lifts of flow categoreis and bimodules which are abstract frameworks for regularizing the moduli spaces. In Subsection
3.5
we lift certain auxiliary structures which will be necessary to carry out the FOP perturbation scheme. In Subsection
3.6
we consider the important notion of stable normal complex structures. In Subsection
3.7
and Subsection
3.8
we explain the recipe of inductively constructing FOP perturbations associated to derived orbifold lifts of flow categories and flow bimodules and the recipe of extracting chain complexes and chain maps from the countings.

3.1.
Stratified spaces

3.1.1.
Partially ordered sets

Many objects in Floer theory are indexed by certain partially ordered sets. We abbreviate the phrase “partially ordered set” by the word
poset
. In this paper posets are always countable. We use different symbols such as
\leq
,
\preceq
, etc. to denote the partial order relations. For a poset
{\mathbb{A}}
, let
{\mathbb{A}}^{\max}\subseteq{\mathbb{A}}
be the subset of maximal elements. A poset
{\mathbb{A}}
has a canonical
Alexandrov topology
: a subset
U\subset{\mathbb{A}}
is open if
\alpha\in U
and
\alpha\leq\beta
imply that
\beta\in U
.

The product of finitely many posets carries a canonically induced partial order. Indeed, if
{\mathbb{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbb{A}}_{k}
are posets, then the relation
