\begin{array}[]{rcl}\delta:{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}&\to&{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{pq}}\\
\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q&\mapsto&\delta(\alpha)=(d_{pr_{1}},\ldots,d_{r_{l}q})\end{array}

(5.2)

such that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{pr}^{\rm Floer}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{rq}^{\rm Floer}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial^{prq}{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{pr}}\times{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{rq}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial^{(d_{pr},d_{rq})}{\mathbb{A}}_{d_{pq}}}

(3)

The
system of extra symmetries
is the collection of compact Lie groups

G_{d}:=\{g\in PU(d+1)\ |\ g([1,0,\ldots,0])=[1,0,\ldots,0]\in\mathbb{CP}^{d}\}\cong U(d).

for all
d\geq 1
. Here
PU(d+1)
acts on
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
in the standard way. The identification with
U(d)
is given by

U(d)\ni g^{\prime}\mapsto\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&g^{\prime}\end{array}\right]\in PU(d+1).

(5.3)

Denote

G_{pq}:=G_{d_{pq}}\ \forall p,q\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\rm Floer},{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(p)<{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(q).

(4)

For each
\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
, there is a group embedding

G_{\delta}:=G_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times G_{d_{l}}\hookrightarrow G_{d}

(5.4)

defined by

\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&g_{0}^{\prime}\end{array}\right],\dots,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&g_{l}^{\prime}\end{array}\right]\right)\mapsto\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&g_{0}^{\prime}&\cdots&0\\
0&\cdots&\ddots&0\\
0&0&\cdots&g_{l}^{\prime}\end{array}\right].

(5)

For each
\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}
, we also denote

G_{\alpha}:=G_{pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q}:=G_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots\times G_{r_{l}q}=G_{\delta(\alpha)}

and identify it with the embedding image in
G_{pq}
. Then whenever
\alpha\leq\beta
there is a group embedding
G_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow G_{\beta}
.

5.1.5.
The main statement

To simplify the notations, through out this section, we use
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
to denote the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}
.

The following definition is the counterpart of Definition
3.22
,
3.24
for K-charts.

Definition 5.8
.

A
weak K-chart presentation (with the system of groups
\{G_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}}
)
of the
{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
-space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
consists of the following objects.

(1)

A collection of K-charts

\Big(K_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha},V_{\alpha},E_{\alpha},S_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})\Big)_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}}

of
(\partial^{\alpha}\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}}
.

(2)

A collection of weak K-chart embeddings

\big\{{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}:K_{\alpha}\rightharpoonup\partial^{\alpha}K_{\beta}\big\}_{\alpha\leq\beta}

They satisfy the following condition.

(1)

The weak K-chart embeddings satisfy the cocycle condition. Namely, for any triple of strata
\alpha\leq\beta\leq\gamma
, one has

{\bm{\iota}}_{\gamma\beta}\circ{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}={\bm{\iota}}_{\gamma\alpha}.

(2)

For each pair of strata
\alpha\leq\beta
, there are a
G_{\alpha}
-equivariant vector bundle
F_{\beta\alpha}\to V_{\alpha}
and a germ of weak open K-chart embedding

{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha}:{\rm Stab}_{F_{\beta\alpha}}(K_{\alpha})\rightharpoonup\partial^{\alpha}K_{\beta}

whose restriction to the zero section coincides with
{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}
. We call
{\bm{\theta}}_{\beta\alpha}
a
stabilization map
.

The weak K-chart presentation is called a
K-chart presentation
if all weak K-chart embeddings are K-chart embeddings.

Definition 5.9
.

A
weak K-chart lift
of the Hamiltonian Floer flow category
T^{\rm Floer}
consists of the following objects.

(1)

A collection of topological K-charts

\big\{K_{pq}=(G_{pq},V_{pq},E_{pq},S_{pq},\psi_{pq})\big\}_{p<q}

of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
.

(2)

A collection of weak K-chart embeddings

\big\{{\bm{\iota}}_{prq}:K_{pr}\times K_{rq}\rightharpoonup\partial^{prq}K_{pq}\big\}_{p<r<q}.

(5.5)

These objects need to satisfy the following conditions.

(1)

When
p=q
,
V_{pp}
is a singleton and
E_{pp}=\{0\}
.

(2)

The weak chart embeddings satisfy the associativity. More precisely, the following diagram commutes,

\textstyle{K_{pr}\times K_{rs}\times K_{sq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial^{prs}K_{ps}\times K_{sq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{pr}\times\partial^{rsq}K_{rq}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial^{prsq}K_{pq}}

where the arrows are defined by (
5.5
).

(3)

For each stratum
\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q\in{\mathbb{A}}^{\rm Floer}_{pq}
, consider the product topological K-chart

K_{\alpha}=(G_{\alpha},V_{\alpha},E_{\alpha},S_{\alpha},\psi_{\alpha})=K_{pr_{1}}\times\cdots K_{r_{l}q}.

The above property implies that for each pair of strata
\alpha\leq\beta
of
pq
, there is a well-defined weak K-chart embedding

{\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha}:K_{\alpha}\rightharpoonup\partial^{\alpha}K_{\beta}.

Then the collection
((K_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}},({\bm{\iota}}_{\beta\alpha})_{\alpha\leq\beta})
form a weak K-chart presentation of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
.

The weak K-chart lift is called a
K-chart lift
if all the weak chart embeddings are chart embeddings.

Notation 5.10
.

We introduce the following notation and convention which are frequently used in this paper. The
G_{pq}
-equivariantization of the product chart
K_{\alpha}
is denoted by

K_{\alpha}^{\sim}:=G_{pq}\times_{G_{\alpha}}(K_{\alpha})=(G_{pq},V_{\alpha}^{\sim},E_{\alpha}^{\sim},S_{\alpha}^{\sim}).

Then usually the version of notations
K_{\alpha}
(or
K_{pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q}
) indicates the objects are constructed from taking products and the version of notations
K_{\alpha}^{\sim}
(or
K_{pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q}^{\sim}
) indicates the objects are constructed from taking products and an equivariantization (by a certain group which is clear from the context).

Now we are ready to state the first main theorem of this section.

Theorem 5.11
.

The Hamiltonian Floer flow category
T^{\rm Floer}
admits a weak K-chart lift.

We also refer to the weak K-chart lift as the “global chart.” This theorem certainly does not contain all the properties we will need in the final construction. Eventually, we need to upgrade the weak lift to a lift and equip it with a smooth structure.

5.2.
Global chart construction I

Following
[
AMS21
, Section 6]
(also in the same spirit as
[
Sie99
, Section 3]
), the goal of this subsection is to introduce a system of auxiliary moduli spaces
(B_{d})_{d\geq 1}
parametrizing stable holomorphic cylinders in
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
, which serve as Deligne–Mumford type moduli spaces for stable Floer trajectories. Moreover, we will consider the gluing of different (stable) holomorphic cylinders from different complex projective spaces. Such consideration will play an important role in the study of global charts of the boundary strata of moduli spaces of (stable) Floer trajectories.

5.2.1.
Moduli spaces of stable cylinders

Following
[
AMS21
]
and
[
AB21
]
, we introduce some auxiliary moduli spaces. Consider a genus zero prestable curve
\Sigma
with two marked points
z_{-}
,
z_{+}
. The two marked points induce a decomposition

\Sigma=\Sigma_{\rm cyl}\cup\Sigma_{\rm sph}

of
\Sigma
into the cylindrical components and spherical components, where the cylindrical components are determined by the vertices on the line connecting
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
in the dual graph. Each cylindrical component
\Sigma_{i}\subset\Sigma
has two special points corresponding to the negative and positive infinities
z_{i,\pm}
. There is a
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
-action on
\Sigma_{\alpha}
, given by biholomorphisms fixing
z_{i,\pm}
.

To fix the rotational gauge on the cylinder, we introduce the following concept. Identifying
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with
S^{1}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, a
lateral line
on a cylindrical component
\Sigma_{i}
is an
{\mathbb{R}}
-orbit
{\rm L}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}
. There are other equivalent notions such as asymptotic markers which can also fix the rotational gauge.

Definition 5.12
.

(1)

A
prestable cylinder
is a pair
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
where
\Sigma
is a genus zero pretable curve with two marked points
z_{-},z_{+}
and
{\bf L}=({\rm L}_{i})
where each
{\rm L}_{i}\subset\Sigma_{i}
is a lateral line on the cylindrical component
\Sigma_{i}
. The cylindrical irreducible components are also referred to as the
horizontal levels
of
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
.

(2)

A
marked stable cylinder
is a triple
(\Sigma,{\bf z},{\bf L})
where
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
is a prestable cylinder and
{\bf z}
is a list of extra marked points which are different from
z_{\pm}
and the nodal points
z_{i,\pm}
, such that each irreducible component is stable (i.e., with at least three special points).

The notion of isomorphisms of marked stable cylinders can be defined in the obvious way. Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}
be the moduli space of marked stable cylinders with
d^{\prime}
marked points. It is a compactification of the moduli space
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}
, the moduli space of marked stable cylinders with
d^{\prime}
marked points with only one cylindrical component and zero spherical components. Forgetting the lateral line defines a smooth
S^{1}
-fibration
{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{2+d^{\prime}}
to the moduli space of genus
0
curves with
2+d^{\prime}
marked points with smooth domains. Following
[
KSV95
, Section 3.2]
,
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}
could be obtained from the Deligne–Mumford space
\overline{{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,2+d^{\prime}}
by performing real blowups along the irreducible components of the normal crossings divisor defined by stable curves with at least two cylindrical components.
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2,d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth manifold with corners: for a more detailed discussion, the reader could refer to
[
LT98b
, Section 2.1]
or
[
AB21
, Section 9.7]
. To simplify the notation, we usually abbreviate
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
as
\Sigma
when there is no ambiguity caused by the context.

5.2.2.
Stable maps to projective spaces

We consider the moduli space of genus zero stable holomorphic maps into
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
with two marked points. Denote by

{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)

the subset of maps whose image is not entirely contained in any hyperplane. Any two smooth curves in
{\mathcal{F}}
can be mapped to another by an element of the symmetry group
PGL(d+1)
of
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
. Let
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
be the universal curve which has an induced
PGL(d+1)
-action.

Lemma 5.13
.

Both
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
and
{\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)
are smooth quasi-projective varieties. Moreover, the evaluation maps at two marked points

{\rm ev}_{+},{\rm ev}_{-}:{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}

are smooth submersions.

Proof.

The first statement follows from
[
AMS21
, Lemma 6.4]
. For the submersive property, note that the natural action of
PGL(d+1)
on
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)
preserves the subspace
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
. Because the linearization of
PGL(d+1)
-action on
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
at any point defines a surjection from its Lie algebra to the tangent space of
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
at this point, the linearization of
{\rm ev}_{\pm}
is surjective as well.
∎

5.2.3.
Stable cylinders in projective spaces

For the purpose of studying Floer theory, one needs to consider the “real” version of the above moduli spaces.

The moduli spaces of stable cylinders in
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
is the set of equivalence classes of objects

(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)

such that
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
is a prestable cylinder and
u:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}
is a stable map, i.e., every constant component has at least three special points. The equivalence relation is defined as follows:
(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)\sim(\Sigma^{\prime},{\bf L}^{\prime},u^{\prime})
if there is an isomorphism
\varphi:(\Sigma,{\bf L})\cong(\Sigma^{\prime},{\bf L}^{\prime})
such that
u=u^{\prime}\circ\varphi
.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{P}^{d},k)
denote the moduli space of stable cylinders in
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
with degree
k
times the generator of
H_{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d};{\mathbb{Z}})
. Define

{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)=\{[\Sigma,{\bf L},u]\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)\ |\ [\Sigma,u]\in{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\}.

Note that the evaluation maps
{\rm ev}_{\pm}
at the two marked points on
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
naturally lift to evaluation maps on
{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
.

Lemma 5.15
.

There exists a natural structure of smooth
{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
-manifold on
{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
satisfying the following conditions.

(1)

The forgetful map
{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
is smooth.

(2)

The evaluation maps
{\rm ev}_{\pm}:[\Sigma,{\bf L},u]\mapsto u(z_{\pm})
are smooth and they are stratified submersive (see Definition
3.19
).

(3)

For any element
a\in{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
represented by
(u,\Sigma,{\bf L})
, choose a generic collection of
d^{\prime}=d(d+2)
hyperplanes and
w_{1},\ldots,w_{d^{\prime}}\in\Sigma
such that
u
intersects with
H_{i}
transversely at
w_{i}
. Then the locally defined map given by taking a stable cylinder to the marked stable cylinder where the marked points are specified by the intersections with these hyperplanes is a diffeomorphism to an open subset of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2+d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

Consider the subspace of
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
which consists of stable holomorphic maps with exactly two cylindrical components of degrees
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
. Denote by the closure of this space in
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
by
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2})
. Then
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2})
is a smooth divisor in the quasi-projective variety
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
. We can consider the real blowup of
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
along
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2})
ranging over all
d_{1}+d_{2}=d
and denote it by
{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
.

We claim that
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
is an
S^{1}
-bundle over
{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
. Indeed, given any stable cylinder
(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)\in{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
with
k
horizontal levels, denote by
\theta_{i}
to be the
S^{1}
-parameter of the lateral line on the
i
-th cylindrical component of
\Sigma
, with the convention that the first component contains
z_{-}
while the
k
-th component contains
z_{+}
. Then the datum

(\Sigma,u,[\theta_{2}-\theta_{1}],\dots,[\theta_{k}-\theta_{k-1}])

defines a point in
{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
because
\theta_{i}-\theta_{i-1}
can be identified with the
S^{1}
-parameter in the exceptional divisor associated to some
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d_{1},d_{2})
. Conversely, given
\theta\in S^{1}
and an element in
{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
, we can define an element
(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)
in
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
by requiring
(\Sigma,u)
to be the image of the blow-down map
{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
and the lateral line
{\bf L}
is defined by the converse process of the previous construction. Note that these constructions are well-defined after choosing local coordinates near each stratum of
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
and they can be patched up together. Using this description, we can view
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
as manifold with corners because
{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
is so. Moreover,
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
is actually a manifold with faces, with codimension-
1
faces corresponding to a partition
d=d_{1}+d_{2}
.

The forgetful map
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
is defined by
(u,\Sigma,{\bf L})\mapsto(u,\Sigma)
, which factors through the projection
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
and the blow-down map
{\mathbb{R}}{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
, therefore it is smooth. The evaluation map
\text{ev}_{+}
(resp.
\text{ev}_{-}
) is the composition of the forgetful map
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
and the ordinary evaluation map
\text{ev}_{+}:{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}P}^{d}
(resp.
\text{ev}_{-}:{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}P}^{d}
), so
\text{ev}_{\pm}
are smooth as well. Accordingly,
\text{ev}_{\pm}
are stratified submersive following the same proof of Lemma
5.13
because the action of
PGL(d+1)
preserves the strata of the simple normal crossings divisor defined by
F_{0,2}(d_{1}+d_{2})
.

For the last statement, it follows from
[
AMS21
, Proposition 6.5]
and the descriptions of
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
and
\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{0,2+d^{\prime}}^{\mathbb{R}}
as
S^{1}
-bundles over real blowups.
∎

5.2.4.
A system of auxiliary moduli spaces

Stable cylinders in projective spaces serve as models of domains of Floer trajectories. Because of the existence of broken Floer trajectories, we need to construct a system of moduli spaces of stable cylinders to capture the information from these boundary strata.

Notation 5.16
.

(1)

Whenever
{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(p)\leq{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(r)<{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(s)\leq{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm Floer}(q)
, embed
\mathbb{CP}^{d_{rs}}
into
\mathbb{CP}^{d_{pq}}
using the map

[z_{0},\ldots,z_{d_{rs}}]\mapsto\big[\ \underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d_{pr}},z_{0},\ldots,z_{d_{rs}},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d_{sq}}\ \big].

(2)

The
system of auxiliary moduli spaces
is the collection of moduli spaces

B_{d}:=\big\{x=[\Sigma,{\bf L},u]\in{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)\ |\ {\rm ev}_{-}(x)=[1,0,\ldots,0]\in\mathbb{CP}^{d}\big\}.

For a partition of
d
given by
\delta
from which
d=d_{0}+\cdots+d_{l}
, we define

B_{\delta}:=B_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times B_{d_{l}}.

Using this notation, define

B_{\alpha}:=B_{\delta(\alpha)},\ {\rm where}\ \alpha\in{\mathbb{A}}_{pq}^{\rm Floer}

where
\alpha\mapsto\delta(\alpha)
is the poset map (
5.2
).

Lemma 5.17
.

B_{d}
is a smooth
{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
-manifold with a smooth
G_{d}
-action.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the blowup description of
{\mathcal{F}}{}_{0,2}^{\mathbb{R}}(d)
as from Lemma
5.15
, the fact that
{\rm ev}_{-}:{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{R}}_{0,2}(d)\rightarrow\mathbb{CP}^{d}
is a stratified submersive, and Lemma
3.20
. ∎

For a partition
\delta\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
, denote by
\partial^{\delta}\mathring{B}
the locally closed smooth
G_{d}
-submanifold of
B_{d}
given by

\partial^{\delta}\mathring{B}:=\partial^{\delta}B\setminus\bigcup_{\delta^{\prime}<\delta}\partial^{\delta^{\prime}}B.

Definition 5.18

(Normalized evaluation)
.

Given
x\in B_{d}
, its
normalized evaluation
at
z_{+}
is a unit vector
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d}
specified as follows. Suppose
\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})
and
x\in\partial^{\delta}\mathring{B}
. Assume that
x
is represented by a stable cylinder
(\Sigma,{\bf L},u)
. Let
\Theta_{i}^{0}
be
i
-th cylindrical component of the domain
\Sigma
and let
u_{i}^{0}
be the restriction of
u
to this cylindrical component. Using the lateral lines, one can identify
\Theta_{i}
with
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
with the lateral line identified with the positive real axis, and with
z_{i,-}
resp.
z_{i,+}
identified with
0
resp.
\infty
. Moreover,
u_{i}^{0}
can be written as

u_{i}^{0}(z)=[f_{i,0}(z),\ldots,f_{i,d}(z)]

where
f_{i,0},\ldots,f_{i,d}
are complex polynomials of degrees at most
d_{i}
. Let
d_{i}^{0}\leq d_{i}
be the maximal degree of these polynomials. We call a list of complex polynomials
(f_{i,0},\ldots,f_{i,d})
a polynomial representative of
u_{i}
. For each
i
, the polynomial representative is only unique up to rescaling by a common factor in
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
and a common reparametrization
z\mapsto\lambda_{i}z
by a positive real number
\lambda_{i}
. However, there exists a unique set of polynomial representatives for all
u_{i}
such that

(1)

(f_{0,0}(0),\ldots,f_{0,d}(0))=(1,0,\ldots,0)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}
.

(2)

For each
i
, the “evaluation” of
(f_{i,0},\ldots,f_{i,d})
at the marked point
z_{i,+}=\infty

(a_{i,0},\ldots,a_{i,d}):=\lim_{z\to\infty}\frac{1}{z^{d_{i}^{0}}}(f_{i,0}(z),\ldots,f_{i,d}(z))

is a unit vector of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}
.

(3)

For each
i\geq 2
,
(f_{i,0}(0),\ldots,f_{i,d}(0))=(a_{i-1,0},\ldots,a_{i-1,d})
.

Then the unit vector
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}):=(a_{l,0},\ldots,a_{l,d})
is called the normalized evaluation of
x\in B_{d}
at
z_{+}
. In particular,

{\rm ev}_{+}(x)=[a_{0},\ldots,a_{d}]\in\mathbb{CP}^{d}.

We denote the normalized evaluation map defined as above by

\widetilde{\rm ev}_{+}:B_{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}.

It is easy to see that
\widetilde{\rm ev}_{+}
is smooth from its construction.

5.2.5.
Boundary strata of the auxiliary moduli spaces

We introduce the following definitions in order to compare the boundary strata of the auxiliary moduli spaces and the products of moduli spaces with lower degrees. This should be thought of as a toy model for the comparison between the restriction of the geometrically-constructed K-charts along the boundary strata and the product of the K-charts from the factorization description of the boundary strata.

Definition 5.19

(Fans and Flags)
.

Fix
d\geq 1
and
\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
.

(1)

A
fan
of type
\delta
is a list of linear subspaces of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}

(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}})

satisfying

(a)

{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}W_{d_{i}}=d_{i}+1
.

(b)

W_{d_{0}}+\cdots+W_{d_{l}}={\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}
.

(c)

{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}(W_{d_{i-1}}\cap W_{d_{i}})=1
.

Let
{\rm Fan}_{\delta}
be the set of all fans of type
\delta
. Then there is a canonical map

\partial^{\delta}B_{d}\to{\rm Fan}_{\delta}

which sends any equivalence class of stable cylinders to the fan for which
W_{d_{i}}
is the subspace such that the image of the
(i+1)
-th level is contained in the projectivization of
W_{d_{i}}
. Note that such a map is well-defined because the stable cylinder at the
(i+1)
-th level has degree
d_{i}
.

(2)

A
flag
of type
\delta
is a list of subspaces of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}

V_{0}\subset\cdots\subset V_{l}={\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}

satisfying

{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{C}}V_{i}=d_{0}+\cdots+d_{i}+1.

Let
{\rm Flag}_{\delta}
be the set of all flags of type
\delta
. Then there is a standard flag of each type
\delta
where

V_{i}={\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+\cdots+d_{i}+1}\times\{0\}^{d_{i+1}+\cdots+d_{l}},i=0,\ldots,l.

(3)

There is a canonical map

{\rm Fan}_{\delta}\to{\rm Flag}_{\delta}

which maps
(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}})
to the flag where

V_{i}=W_{d_{0}}+\cdots+W_{d_{i}}.

(4)

A fan of type
\delta
is said to be
in the normal position
if the following is true. For each
i
, let

\displaystyle\ W_{i}^{-}:=W_{d_{0}}+\cdots+W_{d_{i}},\

\displaystyle\ W_{i}^{+}:=W_{d_{i+1}}+\cdots+W_{d_{l}}.

The condition for being a fan implies that
W_{i}^{-}\cap W_{i}^{+}
is a line
L_{i}
. Let
\mathring{W}_{i}^{\pm}
be the orthogonal complement of
L_{i}
in
W_{i}^{\pm}
(with respect to the standard Hermitian inner product of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d+1}
). Then
\mathring{W}_{i}^{-}
and
\mathring{W}_{i}^{+}
are orthogonal for all
i=0,\ldots,l-1
.

(5)

A point
x\in B_{d}
is said to be in the
\delta
-normal position if
x\in\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
and its associated fan of type
\delta
is in the normal position. Let
(\partial^{\delta}B_{d})^{\rm normal}\subset\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
be the subset of stable cylinders which are in
\delta
-normal position.

We first describe the case of a codimension-one stratum of the auxiliary moduli space as a warm-up. Fix
d\geq 2
and a partition
(d_{0},d_{1})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
. There is a corresponding stratum
\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d}\subset B_{d}
from Notation
5.16
. We would like to define a map

\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}:B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\hookrightarrow\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d}.

(5.6)

Given two arbitrary points
x_{0}\in B_{d_{0}}
,
x_{1}\in B_{d_{1}}
,
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1})
can be defined as follows. Let
u_{0}:\Sigma_{0}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{0}}
,
u_{1}:\Sigma_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{1}}
be representatives. Let
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}})
be the normalized evaluation of
x_{0}
(see Definition
5.18
). One can represent
u_{0}
resp.
u_{1}
as a list of holomorphic sections

(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d_{0}})\ {\rm resp}.\ (g_{0},\ldots,g_{d_{1}})

of the line bundle
L_{0}=u_{0}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(1)\to\Sigma_{0}
resp.
L_{1}=u_{1}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(1)\to\Sigma_{1}
. Then define
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1})\in\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d}
to be the point represented by the map

\displaystyle u_{(d_{0},d_{1})}

\displaystyle:\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},

(5.7)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm[}f_{0}(z),\ldots,f_{d_{0}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{0},\\
{\rm[}a_{0}g_{0}(z),\ldots,a_{d_{0}}g_{0}(z),g_{1}(z),\ldots,g_{d_{1}}(z){\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{1}.\end{array}\right.

Lemma 5.20
.

The map
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}
is equivariant with respect to the group embedding
G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\hookrightarrow G_{d}
from Notation
5.7
.

Proof.

Straightforward from the definitions.
∎

Hence
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}
can be extended to a
G_{d}
-equivariant map

\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}:G_{d}\times_{G_{(d_{0},d_{1})}}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}})\to\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d}.

(5.10)

However, the above map is not surjective as configurations in the image are those stable cylinders whose two levels are in certain “normal” positions.

Lemma 5.21
.

\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}
is bijective onto
(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d})^{\rm normal}
.

Proof.

From the definition we know that the image of
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}
is contained in
(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d})^{\rm normal}
. We first prove the surjectivity. For any
x\in(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d})^{\rm normal}
with associated fan
(W_{d_{0}},W_{d_{1}})
, denote
L_{x}:=W_{d_{0}}\cap W_{d_{1}}
. Let the domain of
x
be
\Sigma=\Sigma_{0}\cup\Sigma_{1}
. By using a unitary transformation on
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
which fixes the point
[1,0,\ldots,0]
, we may assume that
W_{d_{0}}
is spanned by the first
d_{0}+1
coordinates. Then the first level of
x
can be viewed as a stable cylinder in
\mathbb{CP}^{d_{0}}
. Let the normalized evaluation be
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}})
. Then the nodal point of
x
is mapped to
[a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}},0,\ldots,0]
. Then by the definition of being in the normal position,
\mathring{W}_{0}^{+}
is the subspace spanned by the last
d_{1}
coordinates and
W_{d_{1}}=\mathring{W}_{0}^{+}+L_{x}
. Then the second level of
x
is represented by the map

u_{rq}(z)=[a_{0}f_{0}(z),\ldots,a_{d_{0}}f_{0}(z),f_{1}(z),\ldots,f_{d_{1}}(z)]

where
(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d_{1}})
is a list of holomorphic sections of a degree
d_{1}
line bundle over
\Sigma_{1}
. As
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}})
is the normalized evaluation of the first level of
x
, one can see that
x=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1})
where
x_{0}
is the first level of
x
and
x_{1}
is represented by the map
z\mapsto[f_{0}(z),\ldots,f_{d_{1}}(z)]
. Therefore
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}
is surjective.

To prove that
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}
is injective, suppose

x=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}([g,x_{0},x_{1}])=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}([g^{\prime},x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime}]).

We may assume
g^{\prime}=1
. Then the flag associated to
x
is the standard one and
g\in G_{d}
preserves this flag. Hence
g\in G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}
. Hence we may also assume
g=1
. Then
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1})=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0}^{\prime},x_{1}^{\prime})
, which implies
x_{0}=x_{0}^{\prime}
and
x_{1}=x_{1}^{\prime}
. Hence
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{\sim}
is injective.
∎

It is important to prove the associativity of the product construction.

Proposition 5.22
.

For a partition
(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})
of
d\geq 1
,
x_{0}\in B_{d_{0}}
,
x_{1}\in B_{d_{1}}
, and
x_{2}\in B_{d_{2}}
, there holds

\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}(x_{0},\zeta_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{1},x_{2}))=\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}(\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1}),x_{2}).

Proof.

Choose representatives
u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}
of
x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}
respectively. Let

\displaystyle\ (a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}),\

\displaystyle\ (b_{0},\ldots,b_{d_{1}})

be the normalized evaluations of
u_{0}
and
u_{1}
at
z_{+}
. Then by definition,
x_{(d_{1},d_{2})}:=\zeta_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{1},x_{2})
is represented by the map with domain
\Sigma_{1}\vee\Sigma_{2}

u_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm[}u_{1,0}(z),\ldots,u_{1,d_{1}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},\ z\in\Sigma_{1},\\
{\rm[}b_{0}u_{2,0}(z),\ldots,b_{d_{1}}u_{2,0}(z),u_{2,1}(z),\ldots,u_{2,d_{2}}(z){\rm]},\ z\in\Sigma_{2}.\end{array}\right.

Then
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}(x_{0},x_{(d_{1},d_{2})})
is represented by the map with domain
\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}\vee\Sigma_{2}

u_{012}(z)\\
=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\rm[}u_{0,0}(z),\ldots,u_{0,d_{0}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},&z\in\Sigma_{0},\\
{\rm[}a_{0}u_{1,0}(z),\ldots,a_{d_{0}}u_{1,0}(z),u_{1,1}(z),\ldots,u_{1,d_{1}}(z),0,\ldots,0{\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{1},\\
{\rm[}b_{0}a_{0}u_{2,0}(z),\ldots,b_{0}a_{d_{0}}u_{2,0}(z),b_{1}u_{2,0}(z),\ldots,b_{d_{1}}u_{2,0}(z),u_{2,1}(z),\ldots,u_{2,d_{2}}(z){\rm]},&\ z\in\Sigma_{2}.\end{array}\right.

On the other hand,
x_{(d_{0},d_{1})}:=\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1})
is represented by the map
u_{(d_{0},d_{1})}:\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d_{0}+d_{1}}
whose representation is the same as
u_{012}|_{\Sigma_{0}\vee\Sigma_{1}}
above after removing the last
d_{2}
zeroes. Its evaluation at
z_{+}
is represented by the vector

(b_{0}a_{0},\ldots,b_{0}a_{d_{0}},b_{1},\ldots,b_{d_{1}})\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}.

which is a unit vector and is the normalized evaluation of
x_{(d_{0},d_{1})}
. Hence we can see from the definition of
\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}
that the point
\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{(d_{0},d_{1})},x_{2})
is also represented by the map
u^{012}
.
∎

Now given a partition
d=d_{1}+d_{2}+d_{3}
, use (
5.4
), we can define a map

\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{\sim}:G_{d}\times_{G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\times G_{d_{2}}}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}})\to\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}B_{d}

as follows. For any
[g,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]\in G_{d}\times_{G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\times G_{d_{2}}}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}})
, define

\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{\sim}([g,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]):=g(\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}(x_{0},\zeta_{(d_{1},d_{2})}(x_{1},x_{2}))).

It is straightfoward to check that this is a well-defined equivariant map. On the other hand, we can also define a
G_{d}
-equivariant map

[g,x_{0},x_{1},x_{2}]\mapsto g(\zeta_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}(\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}(x_{0},x_{1}),x_{2})).

The following commutative diagram coming from Proposition
5.22

\textstyle{G_{d}(B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{d}\big(B_{d_{0}}\times\partial^{(d_{1},d_{2})}B_{d_{1}+d_{2}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{G_{d}\big(\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1})}B_{d_{0}+d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}}\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\partial^{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}B_{d}}

shows that these two equivariantization maps agree with each other. Therefore, the map
\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{\sim}
is indeed unambiguously well-defined, independent of the ways of grouping the partitions.

It is straightforward to carry out the above discussion to the case with more factors. Recall for
\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
, we defined

B_{\delta}:=B_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times B_{d_{l}}

which has the action of
G_{\delta}
. Using the group embedding (
5.4
), define

B_{\delta}^{\sim}:=G_{d}\times_{G_{\delta}}B_{\delta}.

Then there is a
G_{d}
-equivariant map

\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}:B_{\delta}^{\sim}\hookrightarrow\partial^{\delta}B_{d},

(5.11)

which, for instance, can be constructed by writing
B_{\delta}^{\sim}
as

G_{d}(B_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times_{G_{d_{l-2}}\times G_{d_{l-1}+d_{l}}}(G_{d_{l-1}+d_{l}}\times_{G_{d_{l-1}}\times G_{d_{l}}}(B_{d_{l-1}}\times B_{d_{l}}))).

Proposition
5.22
indicates that such an inductive construction of the map
\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}
is independent of the order of the factorization. This embedding is smooth. The following statement follows from the arguments in the proof of Lemma
5.21
and we record it here.

Lemma 5.23
.

\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}
is bijective onto
(\partial^{\delta}B_{d})^{\rm normal}
. ∎

Lastly we need to analyze the difference between
B_{\delta}^{\sim}
and
\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
. We will show that in fact,
\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
can be viewed as the total space of a
G_{\delta}
-equivariant vector bundle over
B_{\delta}^{\sim}
.

We introduce certain notations which will also be used in the thickening construction. For all
d\geq 0
, denote

{\bm{Q}}_{d}=\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d}/{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\ {\rm where}\ \tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d}:=\Big\{\tilde{h}\in\mathbb{C}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}\ |\ \tilde{h}^{\dagger}=\tilde{h},\ \tilde{h}_{00}\neq 0\Big\}.

(5.12)

Here our convention is that the indices of the Hermitian matrix
\tilde{h}\in\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d}
range from
0
to
d
; the multiplicative group
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
acts on
\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d}
by scalar multiplication on each entry. The
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
-orbit of
\tilde{h}\in\tilde{\bm{Q}}_{d}
is denoted by
[\tilde{h}]
. We identify
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
with

{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{*}:=\Big\{h\in\mathbb{C}^{(d+1)\times(d+1)}\ |\ h^{\dagger}=h,\ h_{00}=0\Big\}

(5.13)

in the way that a Hermitian matrix
h
with
h_{00}=0
is identified with the
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
-orbit of
\tilde{h}=I_{d+1}+h
. Then
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
is a real vector space with dimension equal to
d^{2}+2d
. Moreover, by identifying
G_{d}
with
U(d)\subset U(d+1)
using the correspondence (
5.3
), the usual conjugation action of
U(d+1)
on
(d+1)\times(d+1)
Hermitian matrices restricts to a linear action of
G_{d}
on
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
.

To go further, for each partition
\delta=(d_{0},\ldots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
, define

{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}:=\Big\{[\tilde{h}]\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}\ |\ \tilde{h}_{ij}\neq 0\Longrightarrow\exists\ a\geq-1\ {\rm s.t.}\ d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a}\leq i,j\leq d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a+1}\Big\}

where for
a=-1
, the constraints on the entries are given by
0\leq i,j\leq d_{0}
,
and its complement

\begin{split}\check{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}:=&\ \Big\{[\tilde{h}]\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}\ |\ \tilde{h}_{ij}=0\ \forall a=0,\ldots,l,\ d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a}\leq i,j\leq d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a+1}\Big\}\\
\cong&\ \Big\{h\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{*}\ |\ h_{ij}=0\ \forall a=0,\ldots,l,\ d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a}\leq i,j\leq d_{0}+\cdots+d_{a+1}\Big\}.\end{split}

This vector space is invariant under the adjoint action of
G_{\delta}
. Hence we have a
G_{\delta}
-equivariant splitting

{\bm{Q}}_{d}={\bm{Q}}_{\delta}\oplus{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}.

Moreover,
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
is stratified by partitions. When
\delta\leq\eta
, one has the obvious inclusion

{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}\subset{\bm{Q}}_{\eta}.

Define

{\bm{Q}}_{\eta\delta}:={\bm{Q}}_{\eta}\cap{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}.

Using the
G_{\delta}
-action on the space
{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}
, we define a
G_{\delta}
-equivariant vector bundle

Q_{d,\delta}:=B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}.

The
G_{d}
-equivariantization of this vector bundle is denoted by

Q_{d,\delta}^{\sim}:=G_{d}\times_{G_{\delta}}Q_{d,\delta}\to B_{\delta}^{\sim}.

(5.14)

Proposition 5.24
.

There is a
G_{d}
-equivariant diffeomorphism

\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}:Q_{d,\delta}^{\sim}\cong\partial^{\delta}B_{d}

which extends the embedding
\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}:B_{\delta}^{\sim}\hookrightarrow\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
.

Proof.

We first define a map
\rho_{\delta}:Q_{d,\delta}\to\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
which extends
\zeta_{\delta}:B_{\delta}\to\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
as follows. Given a Hermitian matrix
h\in\check{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}
, we identify it with a complex matrix
\rho_{h}
obtained by changing all entries in
h
that are below the diagonal to zero. Then define

\rho_{\delta}(h,x_{0},\ldots,x_{l}):=(I_{d+1}+\rho_{h})(\zeta_{\delta}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{d})).

Note that
I_{d+1}+\rho_{h}
is a nonsingular matrix because
\rho_{h}
is nilpotent. It is straightforward to check that this map is equivariant with respect to the group embedding
G_{\delta}\to G_{d}
. Hence it extends to a
G_{d}
-equivariant map

\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}:Q_{d,\delta}^{\sim}\to\partial^{\delta}B_{d}.

We need to show that this map is a diffeomorphism.

(1)

(Injectivity) Suppose

x=\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}([g,h,x_{0},\ldots,x_{l}])=\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}([g^{\prime},h^{\prime},x_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{l}^{\prime}]).

Then by the definition of
\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}
, we may assume that
g^{\prime}=1\in G_{d}
. Then, notice that the flag induced by
\rho_{\delta}(h^{\prime},x_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,x_{l}^{\prime})
is the standard one and
g
preserves the flag. Then
g\in G_{\delta}
. Hence we may also assume
g=1\in G_{pq}
. By considering the fans associated to stable maps, one can also conclude that
h=h^{\prime}
. As
I_{d+1}+\rho_{h}
is invertible, one obtains that
x_{i}=x_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i=0,\ldots,l
. Hence
\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}
is injective.

(2)

(Surjectivity) Let
x\in\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
be represented by a map

u=(u_{0},\ldots,u_{l}):\Sigma_{0}\vee\cdots\vee\Sigma_{l}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}.

Let
(V_{0},\ldots,V_{l})
be the induced flag. Then there exists a unitary matrix
g
which transforms this flag to the standard one. Hence we may assume the flag associated to
x
is standard. Let
(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}})
be the fan associated to
x
. We construct an element
h\in{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}
such that

(I_{d_{pq}+1}+\rho_{h})(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}})

is in the normal position.
h
can be constructed inductively. First, let
y_{0}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+1}
be the noramlized evaluation of
u_{0}
at
z_{+}
. Regard
y_{0}
as a vector in
\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}
. Then there exist vectors
w_{1},\ldots,w_{d_{1}}\in\mathbb{C}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}
such that

W_{d_{1}}={\rm span}(y_{0},w_{1},\ldots,w_{d_{1}}).

Then as
W_{d_{0}}={\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+1}
and
W_{d_{0}}+W_{d_{1}}={\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}
, there exists a complex matrix
A_{d_{1}}^{d_{0}}
whose nonzero entries are contained in the upper-right
d_{0}\times d_{1}
-block such that

(I_{d_{pr_{2}}+1}-A_{d_{1}}^{d_{0}})(w_{1},\ldots,w_{d_{1}})=(e_{d_{0}+1},\ldots,e_{d_{0}+d_{1}})

(where
e_{0},\ldots,e_{d_{0}+d_{1}}
form the standard basis of
{\mathbb{C}}^{d_{0}+d_{1}+1}
). Inductively, one can construct a matrix

A:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&A_{d_{1}}^{d_{0}}&A_{d_{2}}^{d_{0}}&\cdots&A_{d_{l}}^{d_{0}}\\
0&0&A_{d_{2}}^{d_{1}}&\cdots&A_{d_{l}}^{d_{1}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0\end{array}\right]

such that
(I_{d+1}-A)(W_{d_{0}},\ldots,W_{d_{l}})
is a fan in the normal position. Apply
I_{d+1}-A
to the stable map
u
, one obtains a stable map
u^{\prime}
which is in the normal position in
\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
. The nilpotence of
A
and its block form imply that
-A=\rho_{h}
for some
h\in{\bm{Q}}_{d,\delta}
. As stable maps in normal positions are all in the image of
\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}
, the above argument implies that
\rho
is surjective.

The smoothness of
\rho_{\delta}^{\sim}
and its inverse follows from the definition and the smoothness of
\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}
.
∎

5.2.6.
Product of the bundle of quadratic forms

The bundles of quadratic forms play an crucial role in the construction of global charts, as they will be part of the obstruction bundle. We describe the multiplicative structures of these bundles. Define for each
d\geq 1
the
G_{d}
-equivariant bundle

Q_{d}:=B_{d}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}.

We would like to define a collection of equivariant bundle embeddings

\textstyle{Q_{d_{0}}\boxplus Q_{d_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q_{d_{0}+d_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{(d_{0},d_{1})}}
\textstyle{B_{d_{0}+d_{1}}}

which are associative. Let us look at the partition
d=d_{0}+d_{1}
and let

Q_{d_{0}\to d},Q_{d_{1}\to d}\to B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\cong B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}

be the bundles
B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}
and
B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}
. Define embeddings

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}:Q_{d_{0}\to d}\to Q_{d},\

\displaystyle\ \widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}:Q_{d_{1}\to d}\to Q_{d}

as follows.

(1)

For
\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}
: for each element
h_{d_{0}}\in{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}
(which is regarded as a Hermitian matrix whose
(0,0)
-entry vanishes), define

\Big(\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}};h_{d_{0}})\Big)_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(h_{d_{0}})_{ij},&\ 0\leq i,j\leq d_{0},\\
0,&\ {\rm otherwise,}\end{array}\right.

which gives a Hermtian matrix in
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
(regarded as a matrix whose
(0,0)
-entry vanishes).

(2)

For
\widehat{\theta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}
: for
x=(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}})
, let the normalized evaluation of
x_{d_{0}}
at
z_{+}
to be
(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}})
. Then there exists an element
g_{x}\in U(d_{0}+1)
such that

g_{x}(0,\ldots,1)=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}).

(5.15)

Embed
g_{x}
into
PU(d_{0}+1)
with image

\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}(g_{x})_{(d_{0}+1)\times(d_{0}+1)}&0\\
0&I_{d_{1}}\end{array}\right]

and denote it by
g_{x}^{+}
. Then for
h_{d_{1}}\in{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}
, define the Hermitian matrix with vanishing
(0,0)
-entry

h_{d_{1}}^{+}\in{\bm{Q}}_{d},\ (h_{d_{1}}^{+})_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(h_{d_{1}})_{i-d_{0}\ j-d_{0}},&\ d_{0}\leq i,j\leq d,\\
0,&\ {\rm otherwise}\end{array}\right.

and define

\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}};h_{d_{1}})=g_{x}^{+}h_{d_{1}}^{+}(g_{x}^{+})^{-1}.

We can easily check that this is independent of the choice of
g_{x}
satisfying (
5.15
).

Then
\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}
and
\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}
canonically correspond to bundle maps, which are denoted by the same symbols respectively. Then define a map

\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q}:Q_{d_{0}\to d}\oplus Q_{d_{1}\to d}\to B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}

to be

\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q}=\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}+\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}.

Lemma 5.25
.

The bundle map
\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q}
is a
G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}
-equivariant linear bundle embedding with image being the bundle
B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}
.

Proof.

The linearity follows from the definition of
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
and the definition of the maps. The
G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}
-equivariance of
\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{0}\to d}^{Q}
is obvious from the definition; the
G_{d_{1}}
-equivariance of
\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}
is also straightforward. However, the
G_{d_{0}}
-equivariance of
\widehat{\zeta}_{d_{1}\to d}^{Q}
requires a verification. Indeed, fix
x=(x_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}})
; suppose the normalized evaluation of
x_{d_{0}}
is
z=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}})
. Then choose
g\in G_{d_{0}}
. Then
gx=(gx_{d_{0}},x_{d_{1}})
and the normalized evaluation of
gx_{d_{0}}
is
gz
. So if
[z]=[a_{0},\ldots,a_{d_{0}}]=g_{x}[0,\ldots,1]
, then
[gz]=gg_{x}[0,\ldots,1]
. Then one can check the
G_{d_{0}}
-equivariance. The injectivity and the fact that the image is exactly
B_{(d_{0},d_{1})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}
is also straightforward to verify following the proof of Lemma
5.21
.
∎

The following proposition is the counterpart of Proposition
5.22
for quadratic bundles, which can be proved based on keeping track of the action of
g_{x}
from (
5.15
).

Proposition 5.26
.

The bundle embeddings defined above are associative. Namely, for
d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}
such that
d=d_{0}+d_{1}+d_{2}
, recall

B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}=B_{d_{0}}\times B_{d_{1}}\times B_{d_{2}}.

Then given
x\in B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}
and

(h_{d_{0}},h_{d_{1}},h_{d_{2}})\in{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{2}},

one has

\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1}+d_{2})}^{Q}(x,h_{d_{0}},\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{1},d_{2})}^{Q}(x,h_{d_{1}},h_{d_{2}}))=\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0}+d_{1},d_{2})}^{Q}(x,\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1})}^{Q}(x,h_{d_{0}},h_{d_{1}}),h_{d_{2}}).\qed

As a consequence, there is a well-defined
G_{d_{0}}\times G_{d_{1}}\times G_{d_{2}}
-equivariant linear bundle embedding

\widehat{\zeta}_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}^{Q}:B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{1}}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{2}}\to B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d},

whose image is
B_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}\times{\bm{Q}}_{(d_{0},d_{1},d_{2})}
. The associativity further implies that for any
\delta=(d_{0},\dots,d_{l})\in{\mathbb{A}}_{d}
, there exists a
G_{\delta}
-equivariant linear bundle embedding

\widehat{\zeta}_{\delta}^{Q}:Q_{\delta}:=B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{0}}\times\cdots\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{l}}\xrightarrow{\sim}B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{\delta}\subset B_{\delta}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}.

(5.16)

Take the
G_{d}
-equivariantization using the group embedding
G_{\delta}\hookrightarrow G_{d}
, we obtain a
G_{d}
-equivariant vector bundle

Q_{\delta}^{\sim}=G_{d}\times_{G_{\delta}}Q_{\delta}\to B_{\delta}^{\sim}

which is
G_{d}
-equivariantly embedded in
B_{\delta}^{\sim}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d}
.
Combined with the gluing map (
5.11
), we obtain a
G_{d}
-equivariant bundle embedding

(\widehat{\zeta}_{\delta}^{Q})^{\sim}:Q_{\delta}^{\sim}\to Q_{d}|_{\partial^{\delta}B_{d}}

covering the embedding
\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}:B_{\delta}^{\sim}\hookrightarrow\partial^{\delta}B_{d}
. Using the bundle
\check{Q}_{\delta}^{\sim}
from (
5.14
), we can stabilize
\zeta_{\delta}^{\sim}
by
\check{Q}_{\delta}^{\sim}
to get a diffeomorphism onto its image.

5.2.7.
Normal complex structure

Here we prove the following result.

Proposition 5.27
.

For each
d\geq 1
,
(B_{d}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d})/G_{d}
is a normally complex orbifold with corners.

Proof.

We first re-examine the relation between
B_{d}
and
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
. Define

{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime}:=\left\{\left.A=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&*\\
0&a\end{array}\right]\ \right|\ a^{\dagger}=a\right\}

Then one can check that
{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime}\cong{\bm{Q}}_{d}
as
G_{d}
-spaces where on both the
G_{d}
action is given by the conjugation. Then we define an “action” of
{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime}
on
B_{d}
by

A\cdot x\mapsto(I_{d+1}+A)(x).

It is easy to see that the
{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\prime}
-orbit through
x
is a local slice of the
G_{d}
-action. Suppose
\Gamma\subset G_{d}
is a finite subgroup and
x\in(B_{d})^{\Gamma}
. Let
{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\Gamma}\subset{\bm{Q}}_{d}
be the
\Gamma
-invariant subspace. Then we see that the
{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\Gamma}
-orbit through
x
is contained in the
\Gamma
-fixed locus. We can check that locally it coincides with the fixed locus. Hence the normal direction to to the
\Gamma
-fixed locus of
B_{d}
and the orthogonal complement of
{\bm{Q}}_{d}^{\Gamma}
are isomorphic representations of
\Gamma
. Hence one can define a natural normal complex structure on
(B_{d}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d})/G_{d}
.
∎

5.2.8.
Abelian gauge theory on punctured spheres

We include certain simple facts about abelian gauge theory over the infinite cylinder.Let
\Theta={\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}
be the infinite cylinder, equipped with the standard flat Riemannian metric. Let
(s,t)
be the standard coordinates where
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
t\in S^{1}
. Consider an everywhere non-negative 2-form

\Omega=\sigma(s,t)ds\wedge dt,\sigma(s,t)\geq 0

which decays exponentially on the ends: there exist a positive real number
\delta>0
and a sequence of positive real numbers
C_{0},C_{1},\ldots
such that

|\nabla^{l}\sigma(s,t)|\leq C_{l}e^{-\delta|s|},\ l=0,1,\ldots.

(5.17)

We assume that

\int_{\Theta}\Omega=d\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}.

Lemma 5.28
.

There exists a unitary connection
A=A_{\Omega}
on the trivial line bundle
L=\Theta\times{\mathbb{C}}
, unique up to gauge transformation, which satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

The curvature form of
A_{\Omega}
is equal to
-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega
.

(2)

The holonomy of
A_{\Omega}
along any circle
\{s\}\times S^{1}\subset\Theta
, which is a well-defined element in
U(1)
, converges to the identity as
s\to\pm\infty
.

Proof.

Choose two integers
m_{-}
and
m_{+}
such that
d=m_{-}-m_{+}
. Choose a reference smooth connection
A_{0}
which is equal to
d-{\bm{i}}m_{\pm}dt
near
\pm\infty
. Consider an arbitrary connection of the form

A=A_{0}+\phi ds+\psi dt.

Then the curvature condition
F_{A}=-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega
and a gauge fixing condition induced by the trivial product connection give the equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial s}-\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}&=*(-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega-F_{A_{0}}),\\[5.69046pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial s}+\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}&=0.\end{array}\right.

(5.18)

The left-hand-side is indeed the standard Cauchy–Riemann operator on
f=\phi+{\bm{i}}\psi
. Introduce a small
\epsilon>0
. Then the operator

\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}:W^{1,p,\epsilon}(\Theta)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\to L^{p,\epsilon}(\Theta)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}

is a Fredholm operator with index
-1
with trivial kernel and 1-dimensional cokernel spanned by a function with nonzero total integral. By the exponential decay property of
\Omega
(see (
5.17
)), when
\epsilon<\delta
, the right hand side of (
5.18
) belongs to
L^{p,\epsilon}
. The choice of the reference connection
A_{0}
implies that the right hand side of (
5.18
) is in the image of the Cauchy–Riemann operator. Therefore there exists a unique solution
f=\phi+{\bm{i}}\psi
of class
W^{1,p,\epsilon}
to (
5.18
). Hence the existence is proved.

For uniqueness, one can see that any other solution
A^{\prime}
differs from
A
by a closed 1-form. Up to gauge transformation, we can assume that the 1-form is
adt
for a constant
a
. Then the holonomy condition forces that
a\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, which is given by the effect of a gauge transformation.
∎

Now we would like to realize
A
as certain singular connection on a degree
d
holomorphic line bundle over
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
. To this end, let
L_{d}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}
be a degree
d
holomorphic line bundle equipped with a Hermitian metric and let
A_{d}
be the Chern connection. Choosing
A_{0}
to be the same reference connection as in the proof of Lemma
5.28
, then over
\Theta=\mathbb{CP}^{1}\setminus\{\pm\infty\}
there exists a trivialization of
L_{d}
such that

A_{d}=A_{0}+\phi_{d}ds+\psi_{d}dt

such that
\phi_{d}
,
\psi_{d}
, and all of their derivatives converge exponentially to
0
as
s\to\pm\infty
. Moreover, the difference of curvature
\Omega_{A_{d}}-\Omega_{A}
, measured in cylindrical metric, decays exponentially like
e^{-\delta|s|}
with all derivatives. Then consider a general complex gauge transformation
g=e^{h}=e^{h^{\prime}+{\bm{i}}h^{\prime\prime}}
where
h^{\prime},h^{\prime\prime}:\Theta\to{\mathbb{R}}
are functions. Consider the equation
(e^{h})^{*}A_{d}=A
which is equivalent to

{\bm{i}}dh^{\prime\prime}+{\bm{i}}*dh^{\prime}=A_{d}-A

which is equivalent to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle{\bm{i}}\left(\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}}{\partial s}-\frac{\partial h^{\prime}}{\partial t}\right)&=\phi_{d}-\phi,\\
\displaystyle{\bm{i}}\left(\frac{\partial h^{\prime}}{\partial s}+\frac{\partial h^{\prime\prime}}{\partial t}\right)&=\psi_{d}-\psi.\end{array}\right.

(5.19)

This is again the standard Cauchy–Riemann operator on cylinder. If we regard the left-hand-side as a Fredholm operator from
W^{1,p,\epsilon}
to
L^{p,\epsilon}
, then it has (complex) index
-1
with the cokernel generated by a function whose total integral is nonzero. Now we allow
h
to have nonzero limits at
\pm\infty
so that
h-h(\pm\infty)
is of class
W^{1,p,\epsilon}
near the infinities. Then the Fredholm index becomes
1
with kernel being the subspace of constant functions. Therefore, there exists a solution to (
5.19
) unique up to adding a constant. We may then view
A
as the Chern connection on
L_{d}
with respect to the Hermitian metric rescaled by
e^{2h^{\prime}}
, which may be a singular metric on
\mathbb{CP}^{1}
but is smooth over the cylinder and continuous over the poles. We summarize these elementary discussions as the following lemma.

Lemma 5.29
.

Given a 2-form
\Omega
as in Lemma
5.28
, there exists a continuous Hermitian metric on
L_{d}
whose Chern connection has curvature form equal to
-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega
. Moreover two such Hermitian metrics differ by a constant.

We generalize the above lemma to the case of prestable cylinders. On a nodal curve, a smooth/holomorphic function or map means a continuous function or map whose pullback to the normalization is smooth/holomorphic. A smooth/holomorphic
k
-form (with
k\geq 1
) means a collection of smooth/holomorphic
k
-forms on its normalization. Then given a genus zero nodal curve
\Sigma
with irreducible components
\Sigma_{1},\ldots,\Sigma_{s}
and integers
d_{1},\ldots,d_{s}
, there exists a unique up to isomorphism smooth/holomorphic line bundle over
\Sigma
whose restriction to
\Sigma_{i}
has degree
d_{i}
. Moreover, the automorphism group of such a holomorphic line bundle is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{C}}^{*}
. Then Lemma
5.29
has the following corollary.

Corollary 5.30
.

(cf.
[
AMS21
, Lemma 6.8]
)
Let
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
be a prestable cylinder and
\Omega
be a smooth 2-form on
\Sigma
. Suppose

(1)

the integration of
\Omega
over each irreducible component is integral, and

(2)

over cylindrical components,
\Omega
satisfies (
5.17
) for some common
\delta>0
.

Then there exists a holomorphic Hermitian line bundle on
\Sigma
whose curvature form is
-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega
. Moreover, this line bundle is unique up to isomorphism.

Under Hypothesis
4.1
, suppose
u:\Sigma\to X
is a smooth map whose restriction to each cylindrical component converges to a periodic orbit of
H
at
\pm\infty
in an exponential rate. Then consider the 2-form
\Omega_{u,H}\in\Omega^{2}(\Sigma)
defined as follows:

\Omega_{u,H}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\displaystyle u^{*}\omega&\text{ on spherical components}\\
\displaystyle u^{*}\omega-d(H_{t}(u)dt)&\text{ on cylindrical components.}\end{array}\right.

(5.20)

Then by Corollary
5.30
there is a unique Hermitian line bundle
L_{u}\to\Sigma
if the integration of the
2
-form
\Omega_{u,H}
is integral over each cylindrical component. In particular, each stable Floer trajectory induces a line bundle over its domain, thanks to Hypothesis
4.1
. The desired integrality property of
\Omega_{u,H}
for stable Floer trajectories follows from the Stokes’ formula and the integrality of symplectic actions. Moreover, the Hermitian line bundle
L_{u}
has strictly positive curvature
2
-forms over the nontrivial components of
u
.

5.3.
Global chart construction II

In this subsection, we provide a construction of a global Kuranishi chart for a single moduli space of Floer trajectories. The purpose is to showcase how to use the auxiliary moduli space
B_{pq}
as a model for the Deligne–Mumford space in the Hamiltonian Floer theory which allows one to present the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
as a global quotient, and to present how to adapt the geometric regularization method from
[
AMS21
]
to this setting.

5.3.1.
Description of the K-chart.

We first define the notion of framed curves in the case of Floer trajectories.

Definition 5.31
.

(cf.
[
AMS21
, Definition 6.10]
)
Given a moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
of Floer trajectories and a stratum
\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q
, denoting
d=d_{pq}
, a
framed cylinder
(of type
\alpha
) is a tuple
(u,\Sigma,F)
where

(1)

\Sigma
is a prestable cylinder with
l+1
horizontal levels (see Definition
5.12
).

(2)

u:\Sigma\to M
is a smooth map whose restriction to each cylindrical component converges to periodic orbits prescribed by the capped orbits
p,r_{1},\dots,r_{l},q
at
\pm\infty
in an exponential rate, and the topological energy of each horizontal level is prescribed by (
4.3
). Moreover, the
2
-form
\Omega_{u,H}
defined in Equation (
5.20
) is non-negative and is strictly positive on each unstable component of
u
.

(3)

F=(f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
is basis of global sections of the line bundle
L_{u}
constructed using the
2
-form (
5.20
). Moreover, the induced holomorphic map

\iota_{F}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},\ w\mapsto[f_{0}(w),\ldots,f_{d}(w)]

(5.21)

is a stable map, which represents a point
[\iota_{F}]\in{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
. Denote by

\tilde{\iota}_{F}:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)

to be the identification between
\Sigma
and the fiber of the universal curve
{\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
over
[\iota_{F}]
.
12
12

12

\tilde{\iota}_{F}
is called a “domain map” in
[
AMS21
]
.

Note that Condition (2) above guarantees the existence of the Hermitian line bundle
L_{u}\to\Sigma
by Corollary
5.30
whose restriction to each unstable component has strictly positive degree. We can define the notion of isomorphisms of framed curves in an obvious way.

Definition 5.32
.

An isomorphism from a framed cylinder
(u,\Sigma,F)
to another framed cylinder
(u^{\prime},\Sigma^{\prime},F^{\prime})
consists of an isomorphism
\varphi:\Sigma\to\Sigma^{\prime}
of prestable cylinders such that
u^{\prime}\circ\varphi=u
and an isomorphism of holomorphic line bundles
\widehat{\varphi}:L_{u}\to L_{u^{\prime}}
which covers
\varphi
and which is an
isometry up to a constant factor
, such that
f_{i}^{\prime}\circ\varphi=\widehat{\varphi}\circ f_{i}
for all
i=0,\ldots,d
. Two framed cylinders are isomorphic if there exists an isomorphism between them. Notice that scaling the frame
F=(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d})
by a nonzero complex number produces an isomorphic framed curve.

Given a framed cylinder
(u,\Sigma,F)
and an element
g\in PGL(d+1)
, we can construct another framed cylinder in the following way. Observe that the sections
f_{0},\dots,f_{d}
define an embedding
\Sigma\to{\mathbb{C}P}^{d}
, under which the line bundle
L_{u}
is the pullback of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
and the basis
(f_{0},\dots,f_{d})
is obtained by pulling back the standard hyperplane sections of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. As an element of the automorphism group of
{\mathcal{C}P}^{d}
, the action of
g
can be lifted to an automorphism of the line bundle
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. Therefore,
g
takes the standard hyperplane sections of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
to another basis of
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
. Composing such an action with the embedding induced by
(f_{0},\dots,f_{d})
defines another framed cylinder and we denote it by

(u,\Sigma,g_{*}F).

(5.22)

We construct a geometric thickening of a moduli space of Floer trajectories using framed cylinders. We choose the following data.

(1)

A Hermitian connection
\nabla^{TM}
on
TM
with respect to the Hermitian metric induced from
\omega
and
J
satisfying the following condition:
\nabla^{TM}
is flat near all 1-periodic orbits (which are all embedded and disjoint by assumption) of
H
and its holonomy along each such orbit is nondegenerate, i.e., does not have
1
as an eigenvalue.

(2)

For each moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
, a positive integer
k
.
13
13

13

Later we will need
k
to be sufficiently large to achieve transversality. We will also need to choose a list of integers instead of a single one to obtain a Kuranishi flow category.

The following definition introduces the thickening induced by one single line bundle. The discussion here is of expository nature, and the actual thickening we need involves multiple line bundles, as presented in detail in Section
5.3.3
.

Definition 5.33

(Thickened moduli)
.

Fix a nonempty moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
. Abbreviate
d=d_{pq}
. We define the following objects.

(1)

The
symmetry group

G_{pq}
is
G_{d_{pq}}
.

(2)

The
thickened moduli space

V_{pq}
parametrizes isomorphism classes of quadruples of the form

(u,\Sigma,F,\eta)

where

(a)

(u,\Sigma,F)
is a framed cylinder. Suppose it is of type
\alpha
for some stratum
\alpha=pr_{1}\cdots r_{l}q
.

(b)

The framing
F=(f_{0},\dots,f_{d})
satisfies

[f_{0}(z_{-}):\cdots:f_{d}(z_{-})]=[1:0:\cdots:0].

(c)

\eta
is an element of the vector space

H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}}.

(5.23)

Here the first
H^{0}
is the kernel of the Cauchy–Riemann operator induced from the Hermitian connection
\nabla^{TM}
on
TM
and the standard complex structure of
\mathbb{CP}^{d}
; on the other hand,
H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}
is the space of holomorphic sections of
\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)\to\Sigma
which vanish at the two marked points
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
.

The quadruple
(u,\Sigma,F,\eta)
needs to satisfy the following perturbation of the Floer’s equation (cf.
[
AMS21
, Equation (6.7)]
): on each component of
\Sigma
one has

\overline{\partial}_{J,H}u+\langle\eta\rangle\circ d\iota_{F}=0,

(5.24)

where
\overline{\partial}_{J,H}u=(du-X_{H}(u))_{J}^{0,1}
in which
X_{H}(u)\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma,u^{*}TM)
is defined by

X_{H}(u):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\displaystyle 0&\text{ on spherical components}\\
\displaystyle dt\otimes X_{H_{t}}&\text{ on cylindrical components,}\end{array}\right.

and the map
\eta\mapsto\langle\eta\rangle
is induced from the Hermitian pairing on
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
.

(3)

The
obstruction bundle

E_{pq}\to V_{pq}
is the direct sum

E_{pq}=O_{pq}\oplus Q_{pq}.

Here

Q_{pq}:=V_{pq}\times{\bm{Q}}_{d_{pq}}

and
{\bm{Q}}_{d}
for all
d\geq 1
was defined in (
5.12
); the fiber of
O_{pq}
at a point
x\in V_{pq}
represented by
(u,\Sigma,F,\eta)
is

H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}}.

(4)

The group
G_{pq}
acts on
E_{pq}\to V_{pq}
(on the left) as follows. Given a quadruple
(u,\Sigma,F,\eta)
where
F=(f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
and
g\in G_{pq}\subset PGL(d+1)
, define the the framed curve

(u,\Sigma,F^{\prime}):=(u,\Sigma,g_{*}F)

as from Equation (
5.22
). Moreover,
g
induces linear isomorphisms

g:H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))\to H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F^{\prime}}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TX)\otimes\iota_{F^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))

and

g:H^{0}(\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}\to H^{0}(\iota_{F^{\prime}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k))_{0}.

The action on the
Q_{pq}
-component of the obstruction bundle is defined as

g\cdot Q\mapsto gQg^{-1},\ g\in G_{pq},\ Q\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}.

(5)

The
Kuranishi map
is

\begin{split}S_{pq}:V_{pq}&\ \to E_{pq}\\
[u,\Sigma,F,\eta]&\ \mapsto(\eta,Q(u,\Sigma,F))\end{split}

where
Q(u,\Sigma,F)
the image of
(d+1)\times(d+1)
Hermitian matrix whose
(i,j)
-entry is

\left[\int_{\Sigma}\langle f_{i},f_{j}\rangle\Omega_{u,H}\right]\in{\bm{Q}}_{d}.

It is easy to see that
Q(u,\Sigma,F)
only depends on the isomorphism class of the framed curve.

(6)

If
[u,\Sigma,F,\eta]\in S_{pq}^{-1}(0)
, one can see that
u:\Sigma\to M
represents an element of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
. Define the
footprint map

\tilde{\psi}_{pq}:S_{pq}^{-1}(0)\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq},\ [u,\Sigma,F,\eta]\mapsto[u]

which induces a continuous map

\psi_{pq}:S_{pq}^{-1}(0)/G_{pq}\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}{}_{pq}.

Remark 5.34
.

Our definition closely follows
[
AMS21
]
. However one difference is that in
[
AMS21
]
, they used the obstruction space

H^{0}(\overline{\rm Hom}(\tilde{\iota}_{F}^{*}T{\mathcal{C}},u^{*}TM)\otimes\tilde{\iota}_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{k})\otimes\overline{H^{0}(\tilde{\iota}_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{k})}

where
T{\mathcal{C}}
is the tangent bundle of the universal curve
{\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)
and
{\mathcal{L}}
denotes a relatively ample line bundle over the universal family
{\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
, and
\tilde{\iota}_{F}
is the domain map,
while we replace this space by ours (
5.23
).

Lemma 5.35
.

\psi_{pq}
is a homeomorphism.

Proof.

See
[
AMS21
, Lemma 6.14]
.
∎

Lemma 5.36
.

For each
x\in S_{pq}^{-1}(0)
, the stabilizer
G_{x}\subset G_{pq}
is isomorphic to the stabilizer of
\psi_{pq}(x)\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
. Moreover, in a
G_{pq}
-invariant open neighborhood of
S_{pq}^{-1}(0)
every point has finite isotropy group.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
AMS21
, Lemma 6.4]
.
∎

Therefore, we see that Definition
5.33
presents
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
as a global quotient preserving the stabilizers. Now we can discuss about the regularity properties.

5.3.2.
Transversality of the perturbed Floer equation

We review Gromov’s graph trick used in the specific setting of global Kuranishi charts in
[
AMS21
]
. Let
(N,J_{N})
be an almost complex manifold and
E\to N
be a Hermitian vector bundle with a Hermitian connection
\nabla^{E}
. Then using the decomposition
TE=T^{h}E\oplus T^{v}E
of the tangent bundle of the total space of
E
into horizontal and vertical tangent bundles induced from
\nabla
, there is an induced “product type” almost complex structure
J^{E}
on
E
by combining
J_{N}
with the fiberwise complex structure on
E
. Now consider a
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear bundle map

\Psi:E\oplus TN\to TN

satisfying

J_{N}(\Psi(e,h))=-\Psi(e,J_{N}(h))

(5.25)

and

\Psi(e,\Psi(e,h))=0.

(5.26)

We define

\Phi:T^{v}E\oplus T^{h}E\to T^{v}E\oplus T^{h}E

whose restriction at each
e\in E
is

\Phi(v,h)=(v,h+\Psi(e,h)).

It is easy to check that

\Phi^{-1}(v,h)=(v,h-\Psi(e,h)).

Define an almost complex structure on
E
by

J_{\Psi}^{E}=\Phi\circ J^{E}\circ\Phi^{-1}:TE\to TE.

More explicitly,

J_{\Psi}^{E}(v,h)=(J^{E}v,J_{N}h+2\Psi(e,J_{N}h)).

Now we consider the Cauchy–Riemann equation with respect to the sheared almost complex structure on the total space, with an additional Hamiltonian perturbation. Let
\Sigma
be a Riemann surface with complex structure
j
. Let
X\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma,{\rm Vect}(N))
be a perturbation. The Hermitian connection on
E
induces a horizontal lift of
X
on the total space, denoted by
X^{E}
. Then consider the Cauchy–Riemann equation for
\tilde{u}:\Sigma\to E

\overline{\partial}_{J^{E}_{\Psi}}\tilde{u}+X^{E}(\tilde{u})=0.

If we write
\tilde{u}=(u,s)
where
u:\Sigma\to N
and
s\in\Gamma(u^{*}E)
, then with respect to the horizontal-vertical decomposition of
TE
, one has

d\tilde{u}=du\oplus\nabla^{E}s

and

\begin{split}(d\tilde{u})^{0,1}_{J^{E}_{\Psi}}=&\ \frac{1}{2}\left(du+J^{E}_{\Psi}\circ du\circ j\right)\oplus(\nabla^{E}s)^{0,1}\\
=&\ \left(\overline{\partial}_{J_{N}}u+X(u)+\Psi(s,J_{N}\circ du\circ j)\right)\oplus(\nabla^{E}s)^{0,1}.\end{split}

Lemma 5.37
.

[
AMS21
, Lemma 6.18]
Let
\Sigma
be a Riemann surface with complex structure
j
and
\tilde{u}=(u,s):\Sigma\to E
be a smooth map. Then
\tilde{u}
corresonds to a smooth map
u:=\pi_{E}\circ\tilde{u}:\Sigma\to N
together with a section
s\in\Gamma(u^{*}E)
. Under this correspondence,
\tilde{u}
is
\tilde{J}_{\Psi}^{E}
-holomorphic if and only if
s
is a holomorphic (with respect to the
(0,1)
-part of
\nabla
) and
u
satisfies

\overline{\partial}_{J_{N}}u+X(u)+\Psi(s,J_{N}\circ du\circ j)=0.\qed

The geometric thickening revisited.

Recall that one has the smooth quasiprojective variety
{\mathcal{C}}={\mathcal{C}}_{0,2}(d)
, which is the universal curve of a submanifold
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)\subset\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{0,2}(\mathbb{CP}^{d},d)
(after imposing the constraint at
z_{-}
by the point
[1,0,\ldots,0]
0. Denote by

{\rm univ}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{F}}

the canonical holomorphic projection map. Also denote by

{\rm ev}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}

the evaluation map. Consider the almost complex manifold

N=M\times{\mathcal{C}}

equipped with the product almost complex structure
J_{N}=J_{M}\times J_{\mathcal{C}}
. Using the projection
p_{M}:M\times{\mathcal{C}}\to M
,
p_{\mathcal{C}}:X\times{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
and the evaluation map
{\rm ev}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}
, define the vector bundles
16
16

16

Again,
k
is chosen to be sufficiently large so that they are vector bundles

\begin{split}E^{0}(k):=&\ \overline{\rm Hom}(({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},p_{M}^{*}TM)\otimes({\rm ev}\circ p_{\mathcal{C}})^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k),\\
E^{1}(k):=&\ p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}{\rm univ}^{*}({\rm univ}_{*}({\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)))_{0}.\end{split}

Here
({\rm univ}_{*}({\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)))_{0}
is the bundle whose fibers are fiberwise global sections of
{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)
which vanish at the two marked points. Define

E(k):=E^{0}(k)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{E^{1}(k)}.

(5.27)

Before talking about the shearing map we also need to specify a Hermitian metric and Hermitian connection on
E
. From the definition we see that all factors of
E
has a natural Hermitian metric and connection except the bundle
({\rm univ}_{*}({\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)))_{0}
which only has a complex structure but no canonical Hermitian structure. We choose an arbitrary one which then induces a Hermitian metric and a Hermitian connection on
E
. This choice does not affect the structures we are going to construct. It is only an auxiliary object to be used to show regularity. The regularity is independent of the choices.

The shearing map
\Psi:E\oplus TN\to TN\cong p_{M}^{*}TM\oplus p_{\mathcal{C}}^{*}T{\mathcal{C}}
is defined to be

\Psi(\eta,v_{1},v_{2})=\langle\eta\rangle(v_{2})\oplus 0.

(5.28)

Here the map
\langle\eta\rangle
is defined at each point
y\in{\mathcal{C}}
(whose evaluation is
z\in\mathbb{CP}^{d}
) with a fiber
{\mathcal{C}}_{y}\subset{\mathcal{C}}
the pairing between
e\in{\mathcal{O}}(k)|_{z}
and the value of
\zeta\in\overline{H^{0}({\mathcal{C}}_{y},{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)|_{{\mathcal{C}}_{y}})_{0}}
using the Hermitian metric on
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
, where we identify
H^{0}({\mathcal{C}}_{y},{\rm ev}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)|_{{\mathcal{C}}_{y}})
with the space of global holomorphic sections on the curve
{\mathcal{C}}_{y}
.

Lift the Hamiltonian.

We need to lift the Hamiltonian vector field to the total space of
E=E(k)
. Let
X_{H_{t}}\in\Gamma(TM)
be the time
t
Hamiltonian vector field on
M
. Then it pulls back to a vector field on
M\times{\mathcal{C}}
which is zero in the
T{\mathcal{C}}
-direction. On the other hand, the Hermitian connection on
E
induces a horizontal-vertical decomposition of
TE
. By identifying the horizontal distribution with the pullback of the tangent bundle of the base, one can lift the vector field
X_{H_{t}}
to the total space
E
, which is denoted by
\tilde{X}_{H_{t}}
. Notice that by the definition of
E
and the choice of the Hermitian connection
\nabla^{TM}
, all 1-periodic orbits in the total space of
E
are contained in the zero section and are exactly the same as the 1-periodic orbits of the original Hamiltonian on
M
multiplied by the additional factor
{\mathcal{C}}
.

An alternative description of the thickened moduli space.

Definition 5.38
.

Let
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
be a prestable cylinder. A smooth map
\tilde{u}:\Sigma\to E
is called a
(\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H)
-holomorphic map if it is
\tilde{J}_{\Psi}
-holomorphic on all sphere components and on each cylindrical components it satisfies the equation

\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial s}+\tilde{J}_{\Psi}\left(\frac{\partial\tilde{u}}{\partial t}-\tilde{X}_{H_{t}}(\tilde{u})\right)=0.

(5.29)

(This is the same as (
5.24
).)

Now we give another description of the thickened moduli space.

Definition 5.39
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}
be the moduli space of
(\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H)
-holomorphic maps
\tilde{u}
from a prestable cylinder
(\Sigma,{\bf L})
to the total space of
E
satisfying the following conditions. Write the projection of
\tilde{u}
to the base of
E
, which is
N=M\times{\mathcal{C}}
, by
(u,\mu)
.

(1)

\mu:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}}
is a domain map, i.e., it is an isomorphism onto a fiber of
{\mathcal{C}}
which sends the marked points to the marked points.

(2)

\tilde{u}
converges to periodic orbits of
\tilde{X}_{H_{t}}
at cylindrical nodes. Moreover, its projection onto
M
has the same homotopy type as elements in
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
.

(3)

For each component
\Sigma_{\alpha}\subset\Sigma
, the degree of the restriction of the underlying map
\underline{\smash{\mu}}:\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d}
is equal to the degree determined by the restriction
u:\Sigma_{\alpha}\to M
.

Notice that there is also a
G_{pq}
-action on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}
.

Now we compare the thickened moduli space
V_{pq}
with
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}
. Indeed, given any point of
V_{pq}
represented by
(\Sigma,u,F,\eta)
, we can identify it with a stable
(\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H)
-holomorphic map
\tilde{u}=(u,\mu,\eta)
where we just replace the frame
F
by the induced map
\underline{\smash{\mu}}=\iota_{F}
. This map is clearly well-defined and
G_{d}
-equivariant.

Lemma 5.40
.

(cf.
[
AMS21
, Lemma 6.25]
The natural map

V_{pq}\to{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}

is an equivariant open embedding (of topological spaces).

∎

Therefore one can identify the obstruction bundle and the Kuranishi map as defined over the space
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}
after restricting to a
G_{pq}
-invariant open subset.

Regularity.

Remember that the thickened moduli space depends on a choice of an integer
k
. To emphasize the role of these integers, we temporarily denote

{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}={\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k).

Theorem 5.41
.

(cf.
[
AMS21
, Corollary 6.27]
)
For a fixed
pq
, there exists a positive integer
k_{pq}>0
such that if
k\geq k_{pq}
, then each element of
S_{pq}^{-1}(0)
is a regular element of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k)
, i.e., the linearization of (
5.29
) is surjective.

Proof.

Choose
x\in S_{pq}^{-1}(0)
. Notice that this element can be viewed as a representative of
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k)
for all
k\geq 1
. We will prove that for sufficiently large
k
,
x
is regular in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k)
. By compactness of the moduli space
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{pq}
, one can find a common large
k
which regularizes all elements of
S_{pq}^{-1}(0)
.

Suppose
x
is represented by the framed cylinder
(\Sigma,u,F,0)
where
F
is a unitary frame of the line bundle
L_{u}\to\Sigma
which is induced from the 2-form
\Omega_{u,H}
whose curvature form is equal to
-2\pi{\bm{i}}\Omega_{u,H}
. The existence of
L_{u}
is guaranteed by Corollary
5.30
. Abbreviate
d=d_{pq}
. Denote the frame
F
by

F:=(f_{0},\ldots,f_{d}).

Then the map

\Sigma\to\mathbb{CP}^{d},\ z\mapsto[f_{0}(z),\ldots,f_{d}(z)]

represents an element in
{\mathcal{F}}_{0,2}(d)
. Therefore, the unitary frame
F
induces a domain map
\mu:\Sigma\to{\mathcal{C}}
. Notice that this construction is independent of
k
. Now for any
k
, denote
(u,\mu):\Sigma\to M\times{\mathcal{C}}
which together with the inclusion map of the zero section of
u^{*}E(k)
gives an element in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\tilde{J}_{\Psi},H}(k)
.

We would like to show that the linearization of Equation (
5.29
) at
x
is surjective when
k
is sufficiently large. We only show its surjectivity over each component, while skipping the argument showing the surjectivity after imposing the matching conditions at nodes (which is the same as the case of pseudoholomorphic curves).

Over each component
\Sigma_{\alpha}\subset\Sigma
, the deformation space of the map
(u,\mu,0)
splits as

W^{1,p}(\Sigma_{\alpha},\mu^{*}T{\mathcal{C}})\oplus W^{1,p}(\Sigma_{\alpha},u^{*}TM)\oplus W^{1,p}(\Sigma_{\alpha},(u,\mu)^{*}E(k))

and the linearization is of the block form

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}D_{\mu}&0&0\\
0&D_{u}&P\\
0&0&D_{E}\end{array}\right]

(5.30)

where the diagonal terms are the standard linearization of the Cauchy–Riemann equation (with or without Hamiltonian perturbation term, depending on whether the component is spherical or cylindrical), and the off-diagonal term
P
is the perturbation of the
TM
-direction (i.e. the inhomogeneous term of (
5.24
)). Notice that the operator
D_{\mu}
is always surjective, reflecting the fact that domain maps to
{\mathcal{C}}
are always unobstructed. This is a classical fact due to the “convexity” of the bundle
\mu^{*}T{\mathcal{C}}
. Hence one only needs to consider the lower-right
2\times 2
-block.

We first consider a spherical component
\Sigma_{\beta}\subset\Sigma
where the Hamiltonian perturbation term is zero. If
\Sigma_{\beta}
has positive degree, then the line bundle
\underline{\smash{\mu}}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)
is a positive line bundle on this component. When
k
is sufficiently large, one can argue in the same way as the case of
[
AMS21
]
to show that the linearized operator is surjective, even after restricting to the subspace where the values at nodes vanish. On the other hand, if
\Sigma_{\beta}
is a ghost component. Then
(u,\mu)^{*}E(k)|_{\Sigma_{\beta}}
is a trivial vector bundle equipped with the trivial Cauchy–Riemann operator. This is the same as the linearized operator in the
TM
-direction. Hence the linear operator is surjective with kernels being constant sections.

Second, we consider a cylindrical component
\Sigma_{\alpha}\subset\Sigma
. We identify
\Sigma_{\alpha}\cong(-\infty,+\infty)\times S^{1}
with cylindrical coordinates
(s,t)
. Using the peak-section argument as in
[
AMS21
]
, one can show that the
TM
-direction is surjective. Hence we only need to show that the operator

D_{E}:W^{1,p}((u,\mu)^{*}E(k))\to L^{p}(\Lambda^{0,1}\otimes(u,\mu)^{*}E(k))

is surjective. When
\Sigma_{\alpha}
is a ghost component (i.e. a trivial cylinder), this surjectivity is obvious (its kernel is zero). When
\Sigma_{\alpha}
is not a ghost component, we need to use the positivity of
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
. One can show that the curvature of
(u,\mu)^{*}E(k)
is everywhere positive when
k
is sufficiently large. Indeed, for
S>0
sufficiently large,
u((-\infty,-S]\cup[S,+\infty))
is contained in a neighborhood of the union of periodic orbits where
\nabla^{TM}
is flat. Hence over the region where
|s|\geq S
, the curvature of
(u,\mu)^{*}E(k)
is pulled back from the universal curve
{\mathcal{C}}
. Hence when
k
is sufficiently large, the curvature is everywhere positive. Then by Lemma
5.42
below,
D_{E}
is surjective on this component.
∎

Lemma 5.42
.

Let
E\to\Theta
be a Hermitian vector bundle and
\nabla
be a Hermitian connection on
E
whose curvature form is everywhere positive definite and whose limiting holonomy has no eigenvalue
1
. Then the Cauchy–Riemann operator

\nabla^{0,1}:W^{1,p}(\Theta,E)\to L^{p}(\Theta,\Lambda^{0,1}\otimes E)

is surjective.

Proof.

We prove by using the maximal principle. Suppose this is not the case. Then there exists
\xi
in the kernel of the formal adjoint of
\nabla^{0,1}
. Using the local coordinate
(s,t)
on
\Theta
we write

\nabla=d+\phi ds+\psi dt.

Let the curvature form of
\nabla
be
Qdsdt
. Then one can identify
\nabla^{0,1}
with the operator

D^{0,1}=\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial s}+\phi\right)+{\bm{i}}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\psi\right).

Then its formal adjoint is
-D^{1,0}
. As
\xi\in L^{q}
, the function
|\xi|^{2}
has a maximal point
z_{0}\in\Theta
. Then

0\geq\Delta|\xi|^{2}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\overline{z}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|\xi|^{2}=|D^{0,1}\xi|^{2}+\langle\xi,D^{1,0}D^{0,1}\xi\rangle=|D^{0,1}\xi|^{2}+\langle\xi,Q\xi\rangle.

As
Q
is everywhere positive, this is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma 5.43
.

When
k
is sufficiently large, the curvature of the connection on
\overline{\rm Hom}(\iota_{F}^{*}T\mathbb{CP}^{d},u^{*}TM)\otimes\iota_{F}^{*}{\mathcal{O}}(k)
is everywhere positive.

Proof.

The curvature is the pullback of the curvatures on the corresponding bundles on the target
X\times\mathbb{CP}^{d}
. Notice that near
\pm\infty
,
u_{M}^{*}TM
is flat. Therefore, the positivity of the curvature of
{\mathcal{O}}(k)
